on $\mathbb R^2$. Its membership in $Y$ forces this constant
to be zero, because
$\int_{\mathbb R^2}\mathsf p_G\,d\xi=\infty$.
Consequently,
\[
f^\nu(\xi)\mathrel{\underset{q,\eta}{\lesssim}} \nu^{-2}e^{-\eta|\xi|^2/4}.
\]
Repeating the same argument with $-f^\nu$ in place of $f^\nu$
gives the opposite bound. Thus
\[
|f^\nu(\xi)|
\mathrel{\underset{q,\eta}{\lesssim}} \nu^{-2}e^{-\eta|\xi|^2/4},
\]
and hence
\begin{equation}\label{eq:regularized-crude-zero-order}
\mathfrak n_{0,\gamma}(f^\nu)
\mathrel{\underset{q,\gamma}{\lesssim}} \nu^{-2},
\qquad 0<\nu\le1.
\end{equation}

~\\
To estimate higher derivatives, write
\[
f^\nu
=
\nu^{-1}(1-\mathcal L)^{-1}
\bigl(q-\Lambda f^\nu\bigr).
\]
For every $k\ge0$ and $0<\gamma<\gamma_1<1$,
\[
\mathfrak n_{k+1,\gamma}(f^\nu)
\stackrel{\substack{\eqref{eq:OU-resolvent-one-derivative}\\
\eqref{eq:Lambda-finite-Gaussian-bound}}}{\mathrel{\underset{n,k,\gamma,\gamma_1}{\lesssim}}} \nu^{-1}
\left(
\mathfrak n_{k,\gamma_1}(q)
+\mathfrak n_{k,\gamma_1}(f^\nu)
\right).
\]
Starting from \eqref{eq:regularized-crude-zero-order} and choosing
an intermediate Gaussian exponent at each application, induction
gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:regularized-derivative-polynomial-loss}
\mathfrak n_{m,\gamma}(f^\nu)
\mathrel{\underset{q,n,m,\gamma}{\lesssim}} \nu^{-(m+2)},
\qquad 0<\nu\le1.
\end{equation}

~\\
We now remove this loss by a finite expansion.
Fix $m$ and define
\[
f_0:=f^0=\Lambda^{-1}q,
\qquad
f_j:=-\Lambda^{-1}(1-\mathcal L)f_{j-1},
\qquad 1\le j\le m+2.
\]
We have the identity
\begin{equation}\label{eq:finite-resolvent-expansion-exact}
\bigl[\nu(1-\mathcal L)+\Lambda\bigr]
\left(
f^\nu-\sum_{j=0}^{m+2}\nu^j f_j
\right)
=
-\nu^{m+3}(1-\mathcal L)f_{m+2}.
\end{equation}
Set \(g:=(1-\mathcal L)f_{m+2}\in Y_n\cap\mathcal Z\).  By
linearity and uniqueness, the difference on the left of
\eqref{eq:finite-resolvent-expansion-exact} is
\(-\nu^{m+3}R_\nu g\).  For this fixed source \(g\),
\[
\mathfrak n_{m,\gamma}
\left(
f^\nu-\sum_{j=0}^{m+2}\nu^j f_j
\right)
\stackrel{\eqref{eq:regularized-derivative-polynomial-loss}}{\mathrel{\underset{q,n,m,\gamma}{\lesssim}}} \nu^{m+3}\nu^{-(m+2)}
=
\nu.
\]
Moreover, since all $f_j$ are fixed and $\nu^j\le\nu$ for $j\ge1$,
\[
\mathfrak n_{m,\gamma}
\left(
\sum_{j=1}^{m+2}\nu^j f_j
\right)
\le
\nu\sum_{j=1}^{m+2}\mathfrak n_{m,\gamma}(f_j)
\mathrel{\underset{q,n,m,\gamma}{\lesssim}} \nu.
\]
Since $f_0=f^0$, the last two estimates imply
\[
\mathfrak n_{m,\gamma}(f^\nu-f^0)
\mathrel{\underset{q,n,m,\gamma}{\lesssim}} \nu.
\]
This proves
\eqref{eq:fixed-mode-strong-fixed-source-convergence}.
Adding $\mathfrak n_{m,\gamma}(f^0)$ gives
\eqref{eq:fixed-mode-higher-fixed-source}.

~\\
For $m=0$ and $\gamma=3/4$,
\[
\|f^\nu-f^0\|_Y^2
\stackrel{\substack{\eqref{eq:Gaussian-space-Y}\\
\eqref{eq:fixed-mode-strong-fixed-source-convergence}}}{\mathrel{\underset{q,n}{\lesssim}}} \nu^2
\int_{\mathbb R^2}e^{-|\xi|^2/8}\,d\xi
\mathrel{\underset{q,n}{\lesssim}} \nu^2,
\]
which proves \eqref{eq:nu-fixed-mode-first-order}.
Taking $m=1$ in
\eqref{eq:fixed-mode-higher-fixed-source} gives
\eqref{eq:nu-fixed-mode-Gaussian}.
<!-- end proof-15 -->
<!-- corollary-2: body_md -->
\label{cor:regularized-finite-dimensional-sources}
Fix \(n\ge2\), a finite-dimensional subspace
\(\mathscr E\subset Y_n\cap\mathcal Z\), and any norm
\(\|\cdot\|_{\mathscr E}\) on \(\mathscr E\). Let \(R_\nu\) be the
solution operator in
Lemma~\ref{lem:regularized-fixed-mode-inverse}.
For every \(m\in\mathbb N_0\) and \(0<\gamma<1\),
\begin{equation}\label{eq:regularized-finite-dimensional-bound}
\mathfrak n_{m,\gamma}(R_\nu q)
+\nu^{-1}\mathfrak n_{m,\gamma}
\bigl(R_\nu q-\Lambda^{-1}q\bigr)
\mathrel{\underset{\mathscr E,n,m,\gamma}{\lesssim}} \|q\|_{\mathscr E},
\qquad q\in\mathscr E,\quad 0<\nu\le1.
\end{equation}
If \(q^\nu\in C^k([0,T];\mathscr E)\), then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:regularized-finite-dimensional-time-bound}
\mathfrak n_{m,\gamma}
\bigl(\partial_t^jR_\nu q^\nu(t)\bigr)
\mathrel{\underset{\mathscr E,n,m,\gamma}{\lesssim}} 
\|\partial_t^jq^\nu(t)\|_{\mathscr E},
\qquad 0\le j\le k.
\end{equation}
<!-- end corollary-2 -->
<!-- proof-16: body_md -->
Choose a basis \(q_1,\dots,q_d\) of \(\mathscr E\) and write
\(q=\sum_{\ell=1}^dc_\ell q_\ell\).  Uniqueness and linearity give
\[
R_\nu q=\sum_{\ell=1}^dc_\ell R_\nu q_\ell,
\qquad
\Lambda^{-1}q=\sum_{\ell=1}^dc_\ell\Lambda^{-1}q_\ell.
\]
The finitely many basis estimates and equivalence of norms give
\[
\begin{aligned}
&\mathfrak n_{m,\gamma}(R_\nu q)
+\nu^{-1}\mathfrak n_{m,\gamma}(R_\nu q-\Lambda^{-1}q)
\\
&\qquad\stackrel{\substack{\eqref{eq:fixed-mode-higher-fixed-source}\\
\eqref{eq:fixed-mode-strong-fixed-source-convergence}}}{\mathrel{\underset{\mathscr E,n,m,\gamma}{\lesssim}}}
\sum_{\ell=1}^d |c_\ell|
\mathrel{\underset{\mathscr E,n,m,\gamma}{\lesssim}} \|q\|_{\mathscr E}.
\end{aligned}
\]
This proves \eqref{eq:regularized-finite-dimensional-bound}.
Since \(R_\nu\) is independent of time,
\[
\mathfrak n_{m,\gamma}(\partial_t^jR_\nu q^\nu)
=\mathfrak n_{m,\gamma}(R_\nu\partial_t^jq^\nu)
\stackrel{\eqref{eq:regularized-finite-dimensional-bound}}{\mathrel{\underset{\mathscr E,n,m,\gamma}{\lesssim}}} \|\partial_t^jq^\nu\|_{\mathscr E},
\]
which is \eqref{eq:regularized-finite-dimensional-time-bound}.
<!-- end proof-16 -->
<!-- proposition-2: body_md -->
\label{prop:fixed-mode-velocity}
Let $n\ge2$ and $f\in Y_n\cap\mathcal Z$. Then $v^f=K*f$ is smooth and, for every $0<\gamma<1$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:fixed-mode-velocity-decay}
|v^f(\xi)|
\mathrel{\underset{n,\gamma}{\lesssim}}
\frac{1}{(1+|\xi|)^{n+1}}
\|f\|_{L^\infty_\gamma},
\qquad \xi\in\mathbb R^2.
\end{equation}
<!-- end proposition-2 -->
<!-- proof-17: body_md -->
The derivative bounds in \eqref{eq:Biot-Savart-finite-derivatives}
ensure smoothness of $v^f$, and
\[
\|v^f\|_{L^\infty}
\stackrel{\eqref{eq:Biot-Savart-finite-derivatives}}{\mathrel{\underset{\gamma}{\lesssim}}} \|f\|_{L^\infty_\gamma}.
\]
This proves the desired estimate on $|\xi|\le2$, after increasing
the constant. We therefore fix $\xi$ with $r:=|\xi|\ge2$.
Recall that
\[
|f(\eta)|
\le
\|f\|_{L^\infty_\gamma}e^{-\gamma|\eta|^2/4}.
\]

~\\
We first show that the angular mode of $f$ forces all moments
of degree less than $n$ to vanish on every disk centered at the
origin. In polar coordinates,
\[
\eta=(\rho\cos\theta,\rho\sin\theta),
\qquad
f(\rho,\theta)
=
a(\rho)\cos(n\theta)+b(\rho)\sin(n\theta).
\]
For a multi-index $\alpha=(\alpha_1,\alpha_2)$,
\[
\eta^\alpha
=
\rho^{|\alpha|}
(\cos\theta)^{\alpha_1}(\sin\theta)^{\alpha_2},
\qquad
|\alpha|=\alpha_1+\alpha_2.
\]
The identities
\[
\cos\theta=\frac{e^{i\theta}+e^{-i\theta}}2,
\qquad
\sin\theta=\frac{e^{i\theta}-e^{-i\theta}}{2i},
\]
show that
$(\cos\theta)^{\alpha_1}(\sin\theta)^{\alpha_2}$
is a finite linear combination of $e^{ik\theta}$ with
$|k|\le|\alpha|$.
If $|\alpha|<n$, then $k+n$ and $k-n$ are both nonzero for
every such frequency. Hence
\[
\int_0^{2\pi}e^{i(k+n)\theta}\,d\theta
=
\int_0^{2\pi}e^{i(k-n)\theta}\,d\theta
=0.
\]
It follows that, for every $\rho>0$,
\[
\int_0^{2\pi}
(\cos\theta)^{\alpha_1}(\sin\theta)^{\alpha_2}
f(\rho,\theta)\,d\theta
=0.
\]
Integrating this identity against
$\rho^{|\alpha|+1}\,d\rho$ on $(0,R)$ gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:fixed-mode-truncated-moments}
\int_{|\eta|\le R}\eta^\alpha f(\eta)\,d\eta=0,
\qquad R>0,\quad |\alpha|<n.
\end{equation}
We next estimate the contribution to the Biot--Savart integral
from $|\eta|\le r/2$.
For every such $\eta$ and every $0\le s\le1$,
\[
|\xi-s\eta|
\ge|\xi|-s|\eta|
\ge r-\frac r2
=\frac r2.
\]
Taylor's theorem therefore applies to $K$ along the segment
joining $\xi$ to $\xi-\eta$:
\[
\frac{d^k}{ds^k}K(\xi-s\eta)
=
(-1)^k
\sum_{|\alpha|=k}
\frac{k!}{\alpha!}\eta^\alpha
\partial^\alpha K(\xi-s\eta),
\qquad
\alpha!:=\alpha_1!\alpha_2!.
\]
Applying the one-dimensional Taylor formula at $s=0$ and
evaluating at $s=1$ therefore gives
\[
K(\xi-\eta)
=
\sum_{|\alpha|<n}
\frac{(-1)^{|\alpha|}}{\alpha!}
\partial^\alpha K(\xi)\eta^\alpha
+\mathcal R_n(\xi,\eta),
\]
where the integral remainder is
\[
\mathcal R_n(\xi,\eta)
=
(-1)^n n
\sum_{|\alpha|=n}
\frac{\eta^\alpha}{\alpha!}
\int_0^1
(1-s)^{n-1}\partial^\alpha K(\xi-s\eta)\,ds.
\]

~\\
The Biot--Savart kernel is homogeneous of degree $-1$:
\[
K(\lambda x)=\lambda^{-1}K(x),
\qquad \lambda>0,\quad x\ne0.
\]
Differentiating with respect to $x$ yields
\[
(\partial^\alpha K)(\lambda x)
=
\lambda^{-1-|\alpha|}(\partial^\alpha K)(x).
\]
Since these derivatives are bounded on the unit circle,
\[
|\partial^\alpha K(x)|
\mathrel{\underset{n}{\lesssim}} |x|^{-n-1},
\qquad |\alpha|=n,\quad x\ne0.
\]
In particular, throughout the segment considered above,
\[
|\partial^\alpha K(\xi-s\eta)|
\mathrel{\underset{n}{\lesssim}} |\xi-s\eta|^{-n-1}
\mathrel{\underset{n}{\lesssim}}  r^{-n-1}.
\]
Since $|\eta^\alpha|\le|\eta|^n$ for $|\alpha|=n$ and
$\int_0^1(1-s)^{n-1}\,ds=1/n$, the remainder satisfies
\[
|\mathcal R_n(\xi,\eta)|
\mathrel{\underset{n}{\lesssim}}  r^{-n-1}|\eta|^n,
\qquad |\eta|\le r/2.
\]

~\\
The moment cancellations eliminate the entire Taylor polynomial:
\[
\begin{split}
&\int_{|\eta|\le r/2}K(\xi-\eta)f(\eta)\,d\eta
\\
&\quad=
\sum_{|\alpha|<n}
\frac{(-1)^{|\alpha|}}{\alpha!}
\partial^\alpha K(\xi)
\int_{|\eta|\le r/2}\eta^\alpha f(\eta)\,d\eta
\\
&\qquad\quad+
\int_{|\eta|\le r/2}
\mathcal R_n(\xi,\eta)f(\eta)\,d\eta
\\
&\quad\stackrel{\eqref{eq:fixed-mode-truncated-moments}}{=}
\int_{|\eta|\le r/2}
\mathcal R_n(\xi,\eta)f(\eta)\,d\eta,
\end{split}
\]
with the truncation radius $R=r/2$.
Consequently,
\[
\left|
\int_{|\eta|\le r/2}K(\xi-\eta)f(\eta)\,d\eta
\right|
\mathrel{\underset{n}{\lesssim}}  r^{-n-1}
\int_{\mathbb R^2}|\eta|^n|f(\eta)|\,d\eta.
\]
The Gaussian bound controls the remaining absolute moment:
\[
\begin{split}
\int_{\mathbb R^2}|\eta|^n|f(\eta)|\,d\eta
&\le
2\pi\|f\|_{L^\infty_\gamma}
\int_0^\infty
\rho^{n+1}e^{-\gamma\rho^2/4}\,d\rho
\\
&\mathrel{\underset{n,\gamma}{\lesssim}} \|f\|_{L^\infty_\gamma}.
\end{split}
\]
We have therefore proved
\[
\left|
\int_{|\eta|\le r/2}K(\xi-\eta)f(\eta)\,d\eta
\right|
\mathrel{\underset{n,\gamma}{\lesssim}} r^{-n-1}\|f\|_{L^\infty_\gamma}.
\]

~\\
It remains to estimate the contribution from $|\eta|>r/2$.
We split this region into $|\xi-\eta|<1$ and $|\xi-\eta|\ge1$.

~\\
On $|\eta|>r/2$, we have
\[
|f(\eta)|
\le
e^{-\gamma r^2/16}\|f\|_{L^\infty_\gamma}.
\]
Since $|K(z)|=(2\pi|z|)^{-1}$ is integrable on the unit disk,
\[
\begin{split}
&\int_{\substack{|\eta|>r/2\\|\xi-\eta|<1}}
|K(\xi-\eta)|\,|f(\eta)|\,d\eta
\\
&\quad\le
e^{-\gamma r^2/16}\|f\|_{L^\infty_\gamma}
\int_{|\xi-\eta|<1}|K(\xi-\eta)|\,d\eta
\\
&\quad\lesssim  e^{-\gamma r^2/16}\|f\|_{L^\infty_\gamma}.
\end{split}
\]
On the complementary region $|\xi-\eta|\ge1$,
the kernel is bounded by $1/(2\pi)$. Moreover,
\[
\int_{|\eta|>r/2}e^{-\gamma|\eta|^2/4}\,d\eta
=
2\pi\int_{r/2}^\infty
\rho e^{-\gamma\rho^2/4}\,d\rho
=
\frac{4\pi}{\gamma}e^{-\gamma r^2/16}.
\]
Therefore,
\[
\begin{split}
&\int_{\substack{|\eta|>r/2\\|\xi-\eta|\ge1}}
|K(\xi-\eta)|\,|f(\eta)|\,d\eta
\\
&\quad\le
\frac1{2\pi}\|f\|_{L^\infty_\gamma}
\int_{|\eta|>r/2}e^{-\gamma|\eta|^2/4}\,d\eta
\\
&\quad\mathrel{\underset{\gamma}{\lesssim}}  e^{-\gamma r^2/16}\|f\|_{L^\infty_\gamma}.
\end{split}
\]
Combining these two estimates gives
\[
\left|
\int_{|\eta|>r/2}K(\xi-\eta)f(\eta)\,d\eta
\right|
\mathrel{\underset{\gamma}{\lesssim}}  e^{-\gamma r^2/16}\|f\|_{L^\infty_\gamma}.
\]
Finally,
\[
\sup_{r\ge2}r^{n+1}e^{-\gamma r^2/16}<\infty,
\]
so this contribution is also
\(\mathrel{\underset{n,\gamma}{\lesssim}}r^{-n-1}\|f\|_{L^\infty_\gamma}\).
Thus
\[
|v^f(\xi)|
\mathrel{\underset{n,\gamma}{\lesssim}} r^{-n-1}\|f\|_{L^\infty_\gamma},
\qquad r=|\xi|\ge2.
\]
Since $r$ and $1+r$ are comparable on $r\ge2$, combining
this estimate with the initial bound on $|\xi|\le2$ proves
\eqref{eq:fixed-mode-velocity-decay}.
<!-- end proof-17 -->
<!-- lemma-15: body_md -->
For every \(\gamma\in(\tfrac12,1)\),
\begin{equation}\label{eq:R0-target}
\left\lVert \mathcal R_0(\cdot,t)\right\rVert_{L^\infty_\gamma}
\mathrel{\underset{\gamma}{\lesssim}} (\nu t)^{\frac{3}{2}},
\qquad
0<t\le T,\quad 0<\nu\le\nu_{0,T}.
\end{equation}
<!-- end lemma-15 -->
<!-- proof-18: body_md -->
Write \(r=\left\lvert \xi\right\rvert\). If \(\sqrt{\nu t}\,r\le1\), then
\[
\begin{aligned}
\Phi(G,v^{E,\nu}_{\text{app}})
&\stackrel{\substack{\eqref{eq:b-multipole}\\\eqref{eq:ABC-def}}}{=}
t\left(A+(\nu t)^{\frac{1}{2}}B+(\nu t) C\right)
-\frac12\sqrt{\frac t\nu}\,r_bG,
\\
\mathcal R_0(\xi,t)
&\stackrel{\eqref{eq:naive-residual-expansion}}{=}
-\frac12\sqrt{\frac t\nu}\,r_b(\xi,t)G(\xi).
\end{aligned}
\]
Hence
\[
\left\lvert \mathcal R_0(\xi,t)\right\rvert
\stackrel{\eqref{eq:b-tail}}{\lesssim} t(\nu t)^{3/2}r^5G(\xi)
\mathrel{\underset{\gamma}{\lesssim}} (\nu t)^{3/2}e^{-\gamma r^2/4}.
\]

~\\
Suppose now that \(\sqrt{\nu t}\,r>1\), equivalently
\(r>(\sqrt{\nu t})^{-1}\). Proposition~\ref{prop:regular-corrector}
supplies the required uniform coefficient bounds, and
\[
\begin{aligned}
\left\lvert \mathcal R_0(\xi,t)\right\rvert
&\stackrel{\substack{\eqref{eq:b-global}\\\eqref{eq:ABC-def}}}{\lesssim} \left(
(\nu t)^{-\frac{1}{2}}r+r^2+(\nu t)^{\frac{1}{2}}r^3+(\nu t)r^4
\right)e^{-\frac{r^2}{4}}
\\
&\lesssim (\nu t)^{-\frac{1}{2}}(1+r)^4e^{-\frac{r^2}{4}}.
\end{aligned}
\]
Since \(r>(\sqrt{\nu t})^{-1}\),
\[
\begin{aligned}
(\nu t)^{-\frac{1}{2}}(1+r)^4e^{-\frac{(1-\gamma)r^2}{4}}
&\mathrel{\underset{\gamma}{\lesssim}} (\nu t)^{-\frac{1}{2}}
e^{-\frac{1-\gamma}{8\nu t}}
\mathrel{\underset{\gamma}{\lesssim}} (\nu t)^{\frac{3}{2}}.
\end{aligned}
\]
Multiplying by \(e^{-\frac{\gamma r^2}{4}}\) proves
\eqref{eq:R0-target} in the outer region.
<!-- end proof-18 -->
<!-- lemma-16: body_md -->
\label{lem:profile-mixed-regularity}
After possibly decreasing \(\nu_{0,T}\), for every \(0<\gamma<1\),
uniformly for \(0\le t\le T\) and
\(0<\nu\le\nu_{0,T}\),
\begin{equation}\label{eq:F-mixed-regularity}
\mathfrak n_{3,\gamma}(F_{\ne0})
+\mathfrak n_{2,\gamma}(\partial_tF_{\ne0})
+\mathfrak n_{0,\gamma}(\partial_t^2F_{\ne0})
\mathrel{\underset{\gamma}{\lesssim}} 1,
\end{equation}
and
\begin{equation}\label{eq:Frad-mixed-regularity}
\mathfrak n_{3,\gamma}(F_{\mathrm{rad}}(\cdot,t))
\mathrel{\underset{\gamma}{\lesssim}} t,
\qquad
\mathfrak n_{1,\gamma}(\partial_tF_{\mathrm{rad}}(\cdot,t))
\mathrel{\underset{\gamma}{\lesssim}} 1.
\end{equation}
Moreover,
\(F_{\mathrm{rad}}(\cdot,t),\partial_tF_{\mathrm{rad}}(\cdot,t)\in\mathcal{Z}\).
The map \(t\mapsto F_{\mathrm{rad}}(\cdot,t)\) is continuous with
respect to \(\mathfrak n_{3,\gamma}\) and \(C^1\) with respect to
\(\mathfrak n_{1,\gamma}\).
<!-- end lemma-16 -->
<!-- proof-19: body_md -->
The estimate \eqref{eq:F-mixed-regularity} is the special case
\((m,j)=(3,0),(2,1),(0,2)\) of
\eqref{eq:F-regularized-space-time-bounds}.  It remains to establish
the assertions for the radial profile.

Set
\begin{equation}\label{eq:Frad-source-N}
\mathcal N(t)
:=
\Pi_{\mathrm{rad}}\left[
\bigl(v^{F_{\ne0}}+\Sigma(t)\xi\bigr)\cdot\nabla F_{\ne0}
\right].
\end{equation}
For any \(0<\gamma_1<\gamma_2<1\),
\eqref{eq:F-mixed-regularity}, \eqref{eq:Biot-Savart-finite-derivatives},
and \eqref{eq:Sigma-time-bound} imply
\begin{equation}\label{eq:Frad-source-space-time-bounds}
\mathfrak n_{2,\gamma_1}(\mathcal N(t))
+\mathfrak n_{0,\gamma_1}(\partial_t\mathcal N(t))
\mathrel{\underset{\gamma_1}{\lesssim}} 1,
\end{equation}
where
\begin{equation}\label{eq:Frad-source-time-formula}
\begin{aligned}
\partial_t\mathcal N
=\Pi_{\mathrm{rad}}\Bigl[
&\bigl(v^{\partial_tF_{\ne0}}+\partial_t\Sigma(t)\xi\bigr)
\cdot\nabla F_{\ne0}
\\
&+\bigl(v^{F_{\ne0}}+\Sigma(t)\xi\bigr)
\cdot\nabla\partial_tF_{\ne0}
\Bigr].
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
The loss from \(\gamma_2\) to \(\gamma_1\) absorbs \(\left\lvert \xi\right\rvert\).

For \(0<u<1\), write
\[
S_u:=e^{(\log(1/u))\mathcal{L}},
\]
and set \(S_1=I\) by continuity.  Changing variables \(s=tu\) gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:Frad-rescaled-Duhamel}
F_{\mathrm{rad}}(t)
\stackrel{\eqref{eq:Frad-Duhamel}}{=}
-t\int_0^1uS_u\mathcal N(tu)\,du.
\end{equation}
Differentiating with respect to time gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:Frad-time-Duhamel}
\partial_tF_{\mathrm{rad}}(t)
\stackrel{\eqref{eq:Frad-rescaled-Duhamel}}{=}
-\int_0^1uS_u\mathcal N(tu)\,du
-t\int_0^1u^2S_u\partial_t\mathcal N(tu)\,du.
\end{equation}
Dominated convergence also gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:Frad-time-trace}
\partial_tF_{\mathrm{rad}}(0)
\stackrel{\eqref{eq:Frad-time-Duhamel}}{=}
-\int_0^1uS_u\mathcal N(0)\,du.
\end{equation}

The commutation identity
\(\nabla e^{\tau\mathcal{L}}=e^{\tau/2}e^{\tau\mathcal{L}}\nabla\)
and \eqref{eq:OU-one-derivative-Gaussian} give, for \(k=1,2,3\),
\begin{equation}\label{eq:OU-higher-derivative-bound}
\left\lVert \nabla^kS_ug\right\rVert_{L^\infty_\gamma}
\mathrel{\underset{\gamma,\gamma_1}{\lesssim}}
\frac{u^{-\frac{k-1}{2}}}{\sqrt{1-u}}\,
\mathfrak n_{k-1,\gamma_1}(g),
\quad 0<u<1,\quad 0<\gamma<\gamma_1<1.
\end{equation}
The endpoint factors are integrable; in particular,
\[
\int_0^1\frac{u}{\sqrt{1-u}}\,du=\frac43,\qquad
\int_0^1\frac{u^{1/2}}{\sqrt{1-u}}\,du=\frac\pi2,\qquad
\int_0^1\frac{1}{\sqrt{1-u}}\,du=2,
\]
and
\[
\int_0^1u^2\,du=\frac13,\qquad
\int_0^1\frac{u^2}{\sqrt{1-u}}\,du=\frac{16}{15}.
\]
Combining these integrals with
\eqref{eq:Frad-source-space-time-bounds} proves
\eqref{eq:Frad-mixed-regularity}.  The corresponding integrable
majorants, together with the continuity of \(\mathcal N\) in
\(\mathfrak n_{2,\gamma_1}\) and of \(\partial_t\mathcal N\) in
\(\mathfrak n_{0,\gamma_1}\), prove continuity of
\(F_{\mathrm{rad}}\) in \(\mathfrak n_{3,\gamma}\) and \(C^1\)
continuity in \(\mathfrak n_{1,\gamma}\), including the trace at
\(t=0\).

Finally, if \(g=Gh\), then
\[
\mathcal{L}(Gh)=G\left(\Delta h-\frac12\xi\cdot\nabla h\right).
\]
Thus \(e^{\tau\mathcal{L}}(Gh)=G\mathcal P_\tau h\), where \(\mathcal P_\tau\) is the
Ornstein--Uhlenbeck semigroup with drift \(-\xi/2\), and
\[
\partial^\alpha\mathcal P_\tau h
=e^{-\frac{\left\lvert \alpha\right\rvert\tau}{2}}\mathcal P_\tau(\partial^\alpha h).
\]
If
\(\left\lvert \partial^\alpha h(\xi)\right\rvert\le M_\alpha(1+\left\lvert \xi\right\rvert)^N\),
the Mehler formula gives a bound in terms of the Gaussian moment
\[
\mathfrak m_N:=\frac1{4\pi}\int_{\mathbb R^2}
e^{-|y|^2/4}(1+|y|)^N\,dy<\infty:
\]
\begin{equation}\label{eq:OU-polynomial-seminorm-bound}
\left\lvert \partial^\alpha\mathcal P_{\log(1/u)}h(\xi)\right\rvert
\le M_\alpha\mathfrak m_N u^{\left\lvert \alpha\right\rvert/2}
\bigl(1+\sqrt u\,\left\lvert \xi\right\rvert\bigr)^N
\le M_\alpha\mathfrak m_N(1+\left\lvert \xi\right\rvert)^N.
\end{equation}
The source \(\mathcal N(t)\) and its time derivative belong to the
class \(\mathcal{Z}\) in \eqref{eq:Gaussian-class-Z}.
For each fixed \(\nu\), both are linear combinations of finitely many
fixed \(\mathcal{Z}\)-profiles with bounded continuous time coefficients.
This follows from the finite-dimensional representation of
\(F_{\ne0}\), \eqref{eq:Frad-source-N}, and
\eqref{eq:Frad-source-time-formula}.
For each fixed \(\nu\), each Gaussian quotient in this finite family
has polynomially growing derivatives by \eqref{eq:Gaussian-class-Z}.
The estimate \eqref{eq:OU-polynomial-seminorm-bound} preserves these
polynomial bounds.
The integrable factors \(u,u^2\) in
\eqref{eq:Frad-rescaled-Duhamel} and \eqref{eq:Frad-time-Duhamel}
then permit differentiation and dominated convergence. Consequently,
\(F_{\mathrm{rad}},\partial_tF_{\mathrm{rad}}\in\mathcal{Z}\), including the
trace at \(t=0\), for each fixed \(\nu\).
The constants in this argument may depend on \(\nu\); the bounds in
\eqref{eq:Frad-mixed-regularity} are uniform.
<!-- end proof-19 -->
<!-- theorem-2: body_md -->
\label{thm:concentrated-approximation-summary}
Let \((w_{2,\mathrm{app}},v_{2,\mathrm{app}})\) be the concentrated
approximation defined in \eqref{eq:high-app}.
For every \(t\in[0,T]\), the angular modes of the profiles satisfy
\begin{equation}\label{eq:profile-mode-membership}
\begin{aligned}
F_{\ne0}(\cdot,t)&\in L^2_{\mathsf{p}_G,2}\cap\mathcal{Z},
&F_{\mathrm{rad}}(\cdot,t)&\in L^2_{\mathsf{p}_G,\mathrm{rad}}\cap\mathcal{Z},
\\
H(\cdot,t)&\in L^2_{\mathsf{p}_G,3}\cap\mathcal{Z},
&J(\cdot,t)&\in
\left(L^2_{\mathsf{p}_G,2}\oplus L^2_{\mathsf{p}_G,4}\right)\cap\mathcal{Z}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
For every \(\frac12<\gamma<1\), uniformly for
\(0\le t\le T\) and \(0<\nu\le\nu_{0,T}\),
\begin{equation}\label{eq:profile-Gaussian-bounds}
\begin{aligned}
&\left\lVert F_{\ne0}\right\rVert_{L^\infty_\gamma}
+\left\lVert \nabla F_{\ne0}\right\rVert_{L^\infty_\gamma}
+\left\lVert F_{\mathrm{rad}}\right\rVert_{L^\infty_\gamma}
+\left\lVert \nabla F_{\mathrm{rad}}\right\rVert_{L^\infty_\gamma}
\\
&\qquad
+\left\lVert H\right\rVert_{L^\infty_\gamma}
+\left\lVert \nabla H\right\rVert_{L^\infty_\gamma}
+\left\lVert J\right\rVert_{L^\infty_\gamma}
+\left\lVert \nabla J\right\rVert_{L^\infty_\gamma}
\mathrel{\underset{\gamma}{\lesssim}} 1,
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
and
\begin{equation}\label{eq:high-profile-velocity}
\begin{aligned}
\left\lvert v^{F_{\ne0}}(\xi,t)\right\rvert
+\left\lvert v^{F_{\mathrm{rad}}}(\xi,t)\right\rvert
+\left\lvert v^J(\xi,t)\right\rvert
&\lesssim
\frac{1}{(1+\left\lvert \xi\right\rvert^2)^{\frac32}},
\\
\left\lvert v^H(\xi,t)\right\rvert
&\lesssim
\frac{1}{(1+\left\lvert \xi\right\rvert^2)^2}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
The approximation itself obeys
\begin{equation}\label{eq:concentrated-approximation-error}
\begin{aligned}
\mathfrak n_{1,\gamma}
\bigl(w_{2,\mathrm{app}}(\cdot,t)-G\bigr)
&\mathrel{\underset{\gamma}{\lesssim}} \nu t,
\\
\sup_{\xi\in\mathbb R^2}
(1+|\xi|^2)^{3/2}
\bigl|v_{2,\mathrm{app}}(\xi,t)-v^G(\xi)\bigr|
&\lesssim \nu t,
\\
\|w_{2,\mathrm{app}}(\cdot,t)-G\|_{L^1}
&\lesssim \nu t.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
<!-- end theorem-2 -->
<!-- proof-20: body_md -->
The assertions on angular modes, Gaussian decay, and velocity follow from
\eqref{eq:F-H-mode-membership},
\eqref{eq:F-H-Gallay-bounds},
\eqref{eq:F-nonzero-velocity-bound},
\eqref{eq:H-velocity-bound},
\eqref{eq:Frad-Gaussian-bound},
\eqref{eq:Frad-velocity-bound},
\eqref{eq:Frad-mixed-regularity},
\eqref{eq:J-mode-and-Gaussian-bound}, and
\eqref{eq:J-velocity-bound}.

For \eqref{eq:concentrated-approximation-error}, set \(s=\nu t\).
The standing choice of \(\nu_{0,T}\) gives \(0\le s\le1\). Hence
\[
\begin{aligned}
\mathfrak n_{1,\gamma}(w_{2,\mathrm{app}}-G)
&\stackrel{\substack{\eqref{eq:high-app}\\
\eqref{eq:profile-Gaussian-bounds}}}{\mathrel{\underset{\gamma}{\lesssim}}} (s+s^{3/2}+s^2)
\mathrel{\underset{\gamma}{\lesssim}} s,
\\
\sup_\xi(1+|\xi|^2)^{3/2}|v_{2,\mathrm{app}}-v^G|
&\stackrel{\substack{\eqref{eq:high-app}\\
\eqref{eq:high-profile-velocity}}}{\lesssim} (s+s^{3/2}+s^2)
\lesssim s.
\end{aligned}
\]
Finally, the integrability of \(e^{-3|\xi|^2/16}\) gives
\[
\|w_{2,\mathrm{app}}-G\|_{L^1}
\stackrel{\eqref{eq:finite-Gaussian-seminorm}}{\le}
\mathfrak n_{1,3/4}(w_{2,\mathrm{app}}-G)
\int_{\mathbb R^2}e^{-3|\xi|^2/16}\,d\xi
\lesssim s.
\]
<!-- end proof-20 -->
<!-- proposition-3: body_md -->
\label{prop:high-residual}
Define
\(\Phi_{\mathrm{app}}(\xi,t)
:=\Phi(w_{2,\mathrm{app}},v^{E,\nu}_{\text{app}})(\xi,t)\).
For every \(\gamma\in(\frac12,1)\), uniformly for
\(0<\nu\le\nu_{0,T}\),
\begin{equation}\label{eq:residual-bound}
\left\lvert \Phi_{\mathrm{app}}(\xi,t)\right\rvert
\mathrel{\underset{\gamma}{\lesssim}} (\nu t)^{\frac32}
e^{-\frac{\gamma\left\lvert \xi\right\rvert^2}{4}},
\qquad
\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{2},\quad 0<t\le T.
\end{equation}
<!-- end proposition-3 -->
<!-- proof-21: body_md -->
Fix \(1/2<\gamma<\gamma_1<\gamma_2<1\).
The profile \(H\) satisfies
\[
\mathfrak n_{2,\gamma_2}(H)
+\left\lVert \partial_tH\right\rVert_{L^\infty_{\gamma_2}}
\stackrel{\eqref{eq:H-unregularized-space-time-bounds}}{\mathrel{\underset{\gamma_2}{\lesssim}}} 1.
\]
Since
\(\left\lVert \xi\cdot\nabla H\right\rVert_{L^\infty_{\gamma_1}}
\mathrel{\underset{\gamma_1,\gamma_2}{\lesssim}} \mathfrak n_{1,\gamma_2}(H)\),
we have
\(\left\lVert \mathcal{L} H\right\rVert_{L^\infty_{\gamma_1}}\mathrel{\underset{\gamma_1}{\lesssim}} 1\).
Hence the evolution terms satisfy
\begin{equation}\label{eq:residual-profile-regularity}
\begin{aligned}
&\left\lVert t\partial_tH\right\rVert_{L^\infty_{\gamma_1}}
+\left\lVert \mathcal{L} H\right\rVert_{L^\infty_{\gamma_1}}
+\left\lVert t\partial_tJ\right\rVert_{L^\infty_{\gamma_1}}
+\left\lVert J\right\rVert_{L^\infty_{\gamma_1}}
+\left\lVert \mathcal{L} J\right\rVert_{L^\infty_{\gamma_1}}
\stackrel{\substack{\eqref{eq:H-unregularized-space-time-bounds}\\
\eqref{eq:J-higher-space-time-bound}}}{\mathrel{\underset{\gamma_1}{\lesssim}}} 1.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}

Set \(s:=\nu t\le1\), and denote the last line of
\eqref{eq:profile-residual-reduction} by \(\mathcal D_{\mathrm{drift}}\).
By \eqref{eq:profile-Gaussian-bounds}, \eqref{eq:high-profile-velocity},
and \eqref{eq:residual-profile-regularity},
\[
|\Phi_{\mathrm{app}}-\mathcal R_0-\mathcal D_{\mathrm{drift}}|
\mathrel{\underset{\gamma}{\lesssim}} s^{3/2}e^{-\gamma|\xi|^2/4}.
\]
Here \(s^2\le s^{\frac32}\), \(t\le T\), and the loss from
\(\gamma_1\) to \(\gamma\) absorbs all polynomial factors in \(\xi\).
The same estimate for \(\mathcal R_0\) is
\eqref{eq:R0-target}.

It remains to estimate the drift term in the last line of
\eqref{eq:profile-residual-reduction}.  If
\(\sqrt{s}\left\lvert \xi\right\rvert\le1\), then
\[
\begin{aligned}
&\left|
\left(
\sqrt{\frac t\nu}\,b-t\Sigma(t)\xi
\right)\cdot
\left[
s\nabla F_{\ne0}+s\nabla F_{\mathrm{rad}}
+s^{\frac32}\nabla H+s^2\nabla J
\right]
\right|
\\
&\qquad\stackrel{\substack{\eqref{eq:b-vector-Taylor}\\
\eqref{eq:profile-Gaussian-bounds}}}{\mathrel{\underset{\gamma_1}{\lesssim}}} t\sqrt{s}\,\left\lvert \xi\right\rvert^2\,
s(1+\left\lvert \xi\right\rvert)^N
e^{-\frac{\gamma_1\left\lvert \xi\right\rvert^2}{4}}
\mathrel{\underset{\gamma}{\lesssim}} s^{\frac32}
e^{-\frac{\gamma\left\lvert \xi\right\rvert^2}{4}},
\end{aligned}
\]
where \(N\) is a fixed integer and \(t\le T\).
If \(\sqrt{s}\left\lvert \xi\right\rvert>1\), then
\[
\left|\sqrt{t/\nu}\,b-t\Sigma(t)\xi\right|
\stackrel{\substack{\eqref{eq:b-global}\\\eqref{eq:Sigma-time-bound}}}{\lesssim} \left(\frac{t}{\sqrt{s}}+t\left\lvert \xi\right\rvert\right).
\]
Therefore,
\[
\begin{aligned}
&\left|
\left(
\sqrt{\frac t\nu}\,b-t\Sigma(t)\xi
\right)\cdot
\left[
s\nabla F_{\ne0}+s\nabla F_{\mathrm{rad}}
+s^{\frac32}\nabla H+s^2\nabla J
\right]
\right|
\\
&\qquad
\stackrel{\eqref{eq:profile-Gaussian-bounds}}{\mathrel{\underset{\gamma_1}{\lesssim}}} 
\left(t\sqrt{s}+ts\left\lvert \xi\right\rvert\right)
(1+\left\lvert \xi\right\rvert)^N
e^{-\frac{\gamma_1\left\lvert \xi\right\rvert^2}{4}}
\mathrel{\underset{\gamma}{\lesssim}} s^{\frac32}e^{-\frac{\gamma\left\lvert \xi\right\rvert^2}{4}}.
\end{aligned}
\]
The last inequality follows from the elementary bound
\begin{equation}\label{eq:outer-Gaussian-absorption}
\sup_{\substack{0<s\le1\\r\ge s^{-1/2}}}
s^{-M}(1+r)^N
e^{-\delta r^2/4}
<\infty,
\qquad M,N\ge0,\quad\delta>0,
\end{equation}
which follows from \(s^{-M}\le r^{2M}\).  Apply it with
\(\delta=\gamma_1-\gamma\).
The estimates on the two regions give
\(|\mathcal D_{\mathrm{drift}}|
\mathrel{\underset{\gamma}{\lesssim}} s^{3/2}e^{-\gamma|\xi|^2/4}\); consequently,
\[
|\Phi_{\mathrm{app}}|
\le |\Phi_{\mathrm{app}}-\mathcal R_0-\mathcal D_{\mathrm{drift}}|
+|\mathcal D_{\mathrm{drift}}|+|\mathcal R_0|
\stackrel{\eqref{eq:R0-target}}{\mathrel{\underset{\gamma}{\lesssim}}} s^{3/2}e^{-\gamma|\xi|^2/4}.
\]
This is \eqref{eq:residual-bound}.
<!-- end proof-21 -->
<!-- theorem-3: body_md -->
\label{thm:linear-weighted-estimate}
Fix \(T>0\), and assume \eqref{eq:linear-b-bounds} and
\eqref{eq:Sigma-time-bound}, with
\(\Sigma(t)^T=\Sigma(t)\) and \(\operatorname{tr}\Sigma(t)=0\).
There exist \(a_0>0\), \(B_0>1\),
\(\nu_T>0\), and \(\kappa_T>0\) such that the following
holds.  If \(0<\nu\le\nu_T\) and \(w\) is a smooth real-valued solution of
\eqref{eq:linearized} satisfying \eqref{eq:zero-mass}, with finite
weighted energy and locally integrable dissipation, then,
for almost every \(0<t\le T\),
\begin{equation}\label{eq:linear-weighted-estimate}
\begin{aligned}
t\frac{d}{dt}E_{\mathsf{p}}(t)
+\kappa_T\mathcal Q(t)
-\left\lvert \left\langle f(t),w(t)\right\rangle_{\mathsf{p}(t)}\right\rvert
&\mathrel{\underset{T}{\lesssim}}tE_{\mathsf{p}}(t).
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
<!-- end theorem-3 -->
<!-- lemma-17: body_md -->
\label{lem:weight-matching}
The function \(\vartheta\) defined in
\eqref{eq:weight-cutoff}--\eqref{eq:weight-cutoff-coefficients}
satisfies
\begin{equation}\label{eq:weight-cutoff-matching}
\begin{gathered}
\vartheta(-1)=\vartheta'(-1)=0,
\\
\vartheta(1)=1-e^{-\frac14},
\qquad
\vartheta'(1)=\frac14e^{-\frac14}.
\end{gathered}
\end{equation}
Consequently, for every \(t>0\), the radial weight
\(\mathsf{p}_0(t,\cdot)\) defined in \eqref{eq:p0-def} is continuous on
\(\mathbb{R}^2\) and belongs to
\(W^{1,\infty}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^2)\).
In particular, the weighted integration by parts identities used below
are valid for \(\mathsf{p}_0\).
<!-- end lemma-17 -->
<!-- proof-22: body_md -->
Fix \(t>0\), and abbreviate \(a=a_\nu(t)\), \(B=B_\nu(t)\),
\(r=|\xi|\), and \(y=r^2-a^2\).  On \([-1,1]\),
\(\vartheta(y)=\zeta_2(y+1)^2-\zeta_3(y+1)^3\) and
\(\vartheta'(y)=2\zeta_2(y+1)-3\zeta_3(y+1)^2\).  Hence
\(\vartheta(-1)=\vartheta'(-1)=0\).  At \(y=1\),
\(\vartheta(1)=4\zeta_2-8\zeta_3\) and
\(\vartheta'(1)=4\zeta_2-12\zeta_3\).  Moreover,
\[
\begin{aligned}
4\zeta_2-8\zeta_3
&\stackrel{\eqref{eq:weight-cutoff-coefficients}}{=}
\left(3-\frac72e^{-\frac14}\right)
-
\left(2-\frac52e^{-\frac14}\right)=
1-e^{-\frac14},
\\
4\zeta_2-12\zeta_3
&\stackrel{\eqref{eq:weight-cutoff-coefficients}}{=}
\left(3-\frac72e^{-\frac14}\right)
-
\left(3-\frac{15}{4}e^{-\frac14}\right)=
\frac14e^{-\frac14}.
\end{aligned}
\]
This proves \eqref{eq:weight-cutoff-matching}.

We next check the matching conditions.  In the Gaussian and transition
regions, \(\mathsf{p}_0(t,r)=e^{r^2/4}(1-\vartheta(y))\), and therefore
\begin{equation}\label{eq:p0-radial-derivative}
\partial_r\mathsf{p}_0(t,r)
=
r e^{\frac{r^2}{4}}
\left(
\frac12(1-\vartheta(y))-2\vartheta'(y)
\right).
\end{equation}

Since \(a\stackrel{\eqref{eq:standing-smallness}}{\ge}2\), the interfaces are
well defined.  At
\(\Gamma_-(t):=\{|\xi|^2=a^2-1\}\), the identities
\(\vartheta(-1)=\vartheta'(-1)=0\) show that both one-sided traces are
\(\mathsf{p}_0=e^{r^2/4}\) and
\(\partial_r\mathsf{p}_0=(r/2)e^{r^2/4}\).

At \(\Gamma_+(t):=\{|\xi|^2=a^2+1\}\), the transition trace is
\(e^{(a^2+1)/4}e^{-1/4}=e^{a^2/4}\), which is the plateau value.
Moreover,
\[
\partial_r\mathsf{p}_0
\stackrel{\substack{\eqref{eq:p0-radial-derivative}\\
\eqref{eq:weight-cutoff-matching}}}{=}
r e^{r^2/4}\left(\frac12e^{-1/4}
-2\cdot\frac14e^{-1/4}\right)=0.
\]
Thus both the value and the radial derivative match at the two
interfaces surrounding the transition annulus.

At the outer interface
\(\Gamma_B(t):=\{|\xi|=B\}\), the plateau and exponential traces are
\(e^{a^2/4}\) and \(e^{\beta B/4}\), respectively.  They coincide
because \(\beta B\stackrel{\eqref{eq:weight-scale-identity}}{=}a^2\).
The radial derivatives need not match:
\(\partial_r^-\mathsf{p}_0(t,B)=0\), whereas
\(\partial_r^+\mathsf{p}_0(t,B)=(\beta/4)e^{\beta B/4}\).  Since \(\mathsf{p}_0\) is continuous, its first distributional derivatives
contain no interface measure. The energy identity uses only these
derivatives; the mixed diffusion term is not integrated by parts again.
Each piece has locally bounded first derivatives, so
\(\mathsf{p}_0(t,\cdot)\in W^{1,\infty}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb R^2)\).
<!-- end proof-22 -->
<!-- lemma-18: body_md -->
\label{lem:weight-cutoff-properties}
For \(-1<y\le1\), the transition cutoff satisfies
\begin{equation}\label{eq:weight-cutoff-properties}
0<\vartheta'(y)
\le
\frac14\bigl(1-\vartheta(y)\bigr).
\end{equation}
<!-- end lemma-18 -->
<!-- proof-23: body_md -->
Direct differentiation gives
\[
\vartheta'(y)
=(y+1)\bigl(2\zeta_2-3\zeta_3(y+1)\bigr),
\qquad
\vartheta''(y)
=2\zeta_2-6\zeta_3(y+1).
\]
By \eqref{eq:weight-cutoff-coefficients}, both derivatives are
positive on \((-1,1]\).  Hence
\(h:=1-\vartheta-4\vartheta'\) is strictly decreasing there.
Moreover,
\(h(1)\stackrel{\eqref{eq:weight-cutoff-matching}}{=}0\), and therefore
\(h(y)\ge0\) for \(-1<y\le1\), which is exactly the upper bound in
\eqref{eq:weight-cutoff-properties}.
<!-- end proof-23 -->
<!-- lemma-19: body_md -->
\label{lem:p0-inner-comparison}
Assume \eqref{eq:standing-smallness}.  For every \(t>0\) and every
\(\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\) with \(\left\lvert \xi\right\rvert^2\le a_\nu(t)^2+1\),
\begin{equation}\label{eq:p0-vs-gaussian}
e^{-\frac{1}{4}}e^{\frac{\left\lvert \xi\right\rvert^2}{4}}
\le
\mathsf{p}_0(t,\xi)
\le
e^{\frac{\left\lvert \xi\right\rvert^2}{4}}.
\end{equation}
<!-- end lemma-19 -->
<!-- proof-24: body_md -->
Set \(y:=\left\lvert \xi\right\rvert^2-a_\nu(t)^2\). In the region under consideration,
\(y\le1\), and
\[
\mathsf{p}_0(t,\xi)
\stackrel{\eqref{eq:p0-def}}{=}
e^{\left\lvert \xi\right\rvert^2/4}\bigl(1-\vartheta(y)\bigr),
\]
and
\eqref{eq:p0-vs-gaussian} is equivalent to
\[
e^{-\frac14}\le1-\vartheta(y)\le1,
\qquad y\le1.
\]

If \(y\le-1\), then
\(\vartheta(y)\stackrel{\eqref{eq:weight-cutoff}}{=}0\), and both
inequalities are immediate.  If \(-1\le y\le1\), then
\(\vartheta'\stackrel{\eqref{eq:weight-cutoff-properties}}{>}0\)
on \((-1,1]\), so \(\vartheta\) is increasing there.  Hence
\[
0
\stackrel{\eqref{eq:weight-cutoff-matching}}{=}
\vartheta(-1)
\le\vartheta(y)\le
\vartheta(1)
\stackrel{\eqref{eq:weight-cutoff-matching}}{=}
1-e^{-\frac14},
\]
which is the required two-sided bound.
<!-- end proof-24 -->
<!-- lemma-20: body_md -->
\label{lem:p0-pointwise-coercivity}
Let \(\mathsf{p}_0\) be the radial weight defined in
\eqref{eq:p0-def}, and let
\begin{equation}\label{eq:chi-def}
\chi_\nu(t,\xi)
:=
\begin{cases}
\left\lvert \xi\right\rvert^2,
&0\le\left\lvert \xi\right\rvert\le a_\nu(t),
\\
a_\nu(t)^2,
&a_\nu(t)\le\left\lvert \xi\right\rvert\le B_\nu(t),
\\
\beta\left\lvert \xi\right\rvert,
&\left\lvert \xi\right\rvert\ge B_\nu(t).
\end{cases}
\end{equation}
Under the smallness assumptions \eqref{eq:standing-smallness}, for
every \(t\in(0,T]\) and almost every \(\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\), one has
the pointwise bound
\begin{equation}\label{eq:p0-pointwise-coercivity}
\frac{\xi\cdot\nabla\mathsf{p}_0}{4\mathsf{p}_0}
-\frac{\left\lvert \nabla\mathsf{p}_0\right\rvert^2}{3\mathsf{p}_0^2}
-\frac{t\partial_t\mathsf{p}_0}{2\mathsf{p}_0}
\ge
\frac1{48}\chi_\nu(t,\xi).
\end{equation}
<!-- end lemma-20 -->
<!-- proof-25: body_md -->
Denote by \(\mathfrak c_0(t,\xi)\) the left-hand side of
\eqref{eq:p0-pointwise-coercivity}, and write \(r=\left\lvert \xi\right\rvert\) and
\(y=r^2-a_\nu(t)^2\).  Let 
\[
\widetilde\vartheta=\vartheta+4\vartheta',
\qquad
\mathcal A(y)=\frac{1-\widetilde\vartheta(y)}{1-\vartheta(y)},
\qquad
\mathcal B(y)=\frac{\vartheta'(y)}{1-\vartheta(y)},
\]
so that \(0\le\mathcal A\le1\), \(\mathcal B\ge0\), and
\(\frac1{48}\mathcal A+\frac14\mathcal B\ge\frac1{48}\).
The standing smallness assumption \(\nu T\le a_0^4/16\) gives
\(a_\nu(t)\ge2\) for \(0<t\le T\), and \(\beta\le1\).
We evaluate \(\mathfrak c_0\) in the four radial regions.

\smallskip
\noindent\emph{1. Gaussian region \(r^2\le a_\nu(t)^2-1\).}
Here \(y\le-1\), so \(\vartheta(y)=\vartheta'(y)=\widetilde\vartheta(y)=0\)
and \(\mathsf{p}_0=e^{r^2/4}\).  Thus
\[
\frac{\nabla\mathsf{p}_0}{\mathsf{p}_0}=\frac\xi2,
\qquad
\frac{t\partial_t\mathsf{p}_0}{\mathsf{p}_0}=0,
\qquad
\mathfrak c_0=\frac{r^2}{8}-\frac{r^2}{12}
=\frac{r^2}{24}=\frac{\chi_\nu}{24}.
\]

\smallskip
\noindent\emph{2. Transition region \(a_\nu(t)^2-1\le r^2\le a_\nu(t)^2+1\).}
Here \(\mathsf{p}_0=e^{r^2/4}(1-\vartheta(y))\), and therefore
\[
\frac{\nabla\mathsf{p}_0}{\mathsf{p}_0}=\frac\xi2\mathcal A(y),
\qquad
\frac{t\partial_t\mathsf{p}_0}{\mathsf{p}_0}=-\frac{a_\nu(t)^2}{2}\mathcal B(y),
\]
so that
\[
\mathfrak c_0
=r^2\left(\frac18\mathcal A(y)-\frac1{12}\mathcal A(y)^2\right)
+\frac{a_\nu(t)^2}{4}\mathcal B(y).
\]
Since \(0\le\mathcal A\le1\), one has
\(\frac18\mathcal A-\frac1{12}\mathcal A^2\ge\frac1{24}\mathcal A\).
Moreover \(a_\nu(t)\ge2\) gives \(r^2\ge a_\nu(t)^2-1\ge a_\nu(t)^2/2\),
while \(\chi_\nu\le a_\nu(t)^2\) throughout this region.  Hence
\[
\mathfrak c_0
\ge
a_\nu(t)^2
\left(\frac1{48}\mathcal A(y)+\frac14\mathcal B(y)\right)
\ge
\frac{a_\nu(t)^2}{48}
\ge
\frac{\chi_\nu}{48}.
\]

\smallskip
\noindent\emph{3. Plateau region \(a_\nu(t)^2+1\le r^2\le B_\nu(t)^2\).}
Here \(\mathsf{p}_0=e^{a_\nu(t)^2/4}\), so \(\nabla\mathsf{p}_0=0\); and since
\(t\partial_ta_\nu(t)^2=-\frac12a_\nu(t)^2\), one has
\(t\partial_t\mathsf{p}_0=-\frac{a_\nu(t)^2}{8}\mathsf{p}_0\).  Therefore
\[
\mathfrak c_0=\frac{a_\nu(t)^2}{16}=\frac{\chi_\nu}{16}.
\]

\smallskip
\noindent\emph{4. Outer exponential region \(r\ge B_\nu(t)\).}
Here \(\mathsf{p}_0=e^{\beta r/4}\) is independent of \(t\), and
\(\nabla\mathsf{p}_0=\frac\beta4\frac\xi r\mathsf{p}_0\).  Hence
\[
\mathfrak c_0=\frac{\beta r}{16}-\frac{\beta^2}{48}.
\]
Since \(\beta B_\nu(t)=a_\nu(t)^2\), we have
\(\beta r\ge a_\nu(t)^2\ge4\), and \(\beta\le1\) gives
\(\beta^2\le\beta\le\frac14\beta r\).  Consequently
\[
\mathfrak c_0
\ge\frac{\beta r}{16}-\frac{\beta r}{192}
=\frac{11}{192}\beta r
\ge\frac{\chi_\nu}{48}.
\]

\smallskip
Combining the four cases proves the claim.
<!-- end proof-25 -->
<!-- lemma-21: body_md -->
\label{lem:q-zero-extension}
Assume \eqref{eq:standing-smallness}.  The function \(q\) defined by
\eqref{eq:q-def} and \eqref{eq:q-initial-extension} is continuous and
locally Lipschitz on \([0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{2}\).  It is \(C^1\) on each smooth
piece determined by the moving interfaces
\(\left\lvert \xi\right\rvert^2=a_\nu(t)^2\pm1\), and its first derivatives are locally
bounded on those pieces.  More precisely, for every \(L>0\),
\begin{equation}\label{eq:q-local-Lipschitz}
\sup_{\substack{0<t\le T,\ \left\lvert \xi\right\rvert\le L\\
\left\lvert \xi\right\rvert^2\ne a_\nu(t)^2\pm1}}
\left(
\left\lvert q(t,\xi)\right\rvert
+\left\lvert \nabla q(t,\xi)\right\rvert
+\left\lvert \partial_tq(t,\xi)\right\rvert
\right)
<\infty.
\end{equation}
Moreover,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:q-boundary-trace}
q(t,\xi)=0
\qquad
\text{whenever }
0<t\le T
\quad\text{and}\quad
\left\lvert \xi\right\rvert^2=a_\nu(t)^2+1.
\end{equation}
<!-- end lemma-21 -->
<!-- proof-26: body_md -->
The function \(\mathfrak h\) is smooth on \([0,\infty)\).  Writing
\(s=\left\lvert \xi\right\rvert^2\) and \(y=s-a_\nu(t)^2\),
\eqref{eq:p0-gradient}, \eqref{eq:q-def}, and \eqref{eq:h-def} give
\begin{equation}\label{eq:q-compact-form}
q(t,\xi)
=
\pi\mathfrak h(s)
\bigl(1-\widetilde\vartheta(y)\bigr)
\bigl(\xi\cdot\Sigma(t)\xi^\perp\bigr),
\qquad y\le1,
\end{equation}
while \(q(t,\xi)=0\) for \(y>1\).  Since the last factor is quadratic
in \(\xi\), and
\(a_\nu(t)\stackrel{\eqref{eq:standing-smallness}}{\ge}2\),
\eqref{eq:q-compact-form} also proves that the value
\(q(t,0)=0\) removes the apparent singularity at the origin.

The endpoint values are
\[
\widetilde\vartheta(-1)
\stackrel{\substack{\eqref{eq:vartheta-tilde-def}\\
\eqref{eq:weight-cutoff-matching}}}{=}0,
\qquad
\widetilde\vartheta(1)
\stackrel{\substack{\eqref{eq:vartheta-tilde-def}\\
\eqref{eq:weight-cutoff-matching}}}{=}1.
\]
Hence the traces in
\eqref{eq:q-compact-form} match at \(y=-1\), and the inner trace at
\(y=1\) equals the exterior value zero.  Thus \(q\) is continuous
across both moving interfaces, and \eqref{eq:q-boundary-trace} follows.

Fix \(L>0\).  Since \(a_\nu(t)\to\infty\) as \(t\to0\), there
exists \(t_L\in(0,T]\) such that \(y\le-1\) whenever
\(0<t\le t_L\) and \(\left\lvert \xi\right\rvert\le L\).  On this set,
\[
q(t,\xi)
\stackrel{\eqref{eq:q-compact-form}}{=}
\pi\mathfrak h(\left\lvert \xi\right\rvert^2)
\bigl(\xi\cdot\Sigma(t)\xi^\perp\bigr),
\]
whose value at \(t=0\) is precisely \eqref{eq:q-initial-extension}.
It therefore gives a \(C^1\) extension with bounded first derivatives
on \([0,t_L]\times\{\left\lvert \xi\right\rvert\le L\}\).

On \([t_L,T]\times\{\left\lvert \xi\right\rvert\le L\}\), formula
\eqref{eq:q-compact-form} is \(C^1\) on each smooth piece and has
bounded one-sided first derivatives.  Together with the matching
traces proved above, this yields local Lipschitz continuity across the
two smooth interfaces and proves \eqref{eq:q-local-Lipschitz}.
<!-- end proof-26 -->
<!-- lemma-22: body_md -->
\label{lem:q-cell}
For every \(t\in(0,T]\), one has, for almost every
\(\xi\in\{\left\lvert \xi\right\rvert^2\le a_\nu(t)^2+1\}\),
\begin{equation}\label{eq:q-cell}
v^G(\xi)\cdot\nabla q(t,\xi)
=
-\Sigma(t)\xi\cdot\nabla\mathsf{p}_0(t,\xi).
\end{equation}
<!-- end lemma-22 -->
<!-- proof-27: body_md -->
Because \(\mathsf{p}_0\) is radial,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:Sigma-radial-reduction}
\Sigma\xi\cdot\nabla\mathsf{p}_0
=
\frac{\xi\cdot\nabla\mathsf{p}_0}{\left\lvert \xi\right\rvert^2}
(\xi\cdot\Sigma\xi).
\end{equation}
Since \(\Sigma\) is symmetric and trace free,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:angular-strain-identity}
\partial_\theta
\bigl(\xi\cdot\Sigma\xi^\perp\bigr)
=
-2\xi\cdot\Sigma\xi,
\qquad
\partial_\theta:=\xi^\perp\cdot\nabla.
\end{equation}
Also,
\(v^G\cdot\nabla
=(1-e^{-\left\lvert \xi\right\rvert^2/4})(2\pi\left\lvert \xi\right\rvert^2)^{-1}\partial_\theta\).
Therefore, for almost every \(\xi\) in this region,
\[ v^G\cdot\nabla q \stackrel{\substack{\eqref{eq:q-def}\\ \eqref{eq:angular-strain-identity}}}{=} -\frac{\xi\cdot\nabla\mathsf{p}_0}{\left\lvert \xi\right\rvert^2}(\xi\cdot\Sigma\xi) \stackrel{\eqref{eq:Sigma-radial-reduction}}{=} -\Sigma\xi\cdot\nabla\mathsf{p}_0, \]which is \eqref{eq:q-cell}.
<!-- end proof-27 -->
<!-- lemma-23: body_md -->
\label{lem:adapted-weight}
For all \(0<t\le T\) and
\(\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\),
\begin{equation}\label{eq:q-pointwise}
\left\lvert q\right\rvert
\mathrel{\underset{T}{\lesssim}}
\left\lvert \xi\right\rvert^2(1+\left\lvert \xi\right\rvert^2)\mathsf{p}_0,
\end{equation}
and, for every \(0<t\le T\) and almost every \(\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\),
\begin{equation}\label{eq:q-derivative-pointwise}
\left\lvert \xi\right\rvert\left\lvert \nabla q\right\rvert
+\left\lvert t\partial_tq\right\rvert
\mathrel{\underset{T}{\lesssim}}
\left\lvert \xi\right\rvert^2(1+\left\lvert \xi\right\rvert^2)^2\mathsf{p}_0.
\end{equation}
Consequently,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:normalized-q-bounds}
\begin{aligned}
(\nu t)\frac{\left\lvert q\right\rvert}{\mathsf{p}_0}
&\mathrel{\underset{T}{\lesssim}}
a_0^4,
\\
(\nu t)
\frac{
  \left\lvert \xi\right\rvert\left\lvert \nabla q\right\rvert
  +\left\lvert q+t\partial_tq\right\rvert
}{\mathsf{p}_0}
&\mathrel{\underset{T}{\lesssim}}
a_0^4\chi_\nu(t,\xi).
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
For every \(0<t\le T\), the first line of
\eqref{eq:normalized-q-bounds} holds everywhere in \(\xi\), and the
second almost everywhere in \(\xi\).
After decreasing \(a_0\), if necessary, one also has
\begin{equation}\label{eq:p-comparable}
\frac34\mathsf{p}_0
\le
\mathsf{p}
\le
\frac54\mathsf{p}_0.
\end{equation}
Moreover,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:p-global-bounds}
\frac12e^{\frac{\beta\left\lvert \xi\right\rvert}{4}}
\le
\mathsf{p}(t,\xi)
\le
2e^{\frac{\left\lvert \xi\right\rvert^2}{4}}.
\end{equation}
<!-- end lemma-23 -->
<!-- proof-28: body_md -->
Write
\[
r=\left\lvert \xi\right\rvert,
\qquad
s=r^2,
\qquad
y=s-a_\nu(t)^2.\]
We first prove the pointwise estimates.  The function \(\mathfrak h\)
is smooth at \(s=0\), and
\begin{equation}\label{eq:h-growth-bounds}
\left\lvert \mathfrak h(s)\right\rvert
\stackrel{\eqref{eq:h-def}}{\lesssim}
(1+s)e^{s/4},
\qquad
s\left\lvert \mathfrak h'(s)\right\rvert
\stackrel{\eqref{eq:h-def}}{\lesssim}
s(1+s)e^{s/4}.
\end{equation}
Indeed, these bounds follow from smoothness on \(0\le s\le1\), while
for \(s\ge1\) the denominator \(1-e^{-s/4}\) is bounded away from zero
and direct differentiation applies.

On \(y\le1\),
\[
0\le1-\widetilde\vartheta(y)
\stackrel{\eqref{eq:weight-cutoff-properties}}{\le}
1-\vartheta(y).
\]
Moreover, on each smooth piece,
\[
\left\lvert \partial_y(1-\widetilde\vartheta(y))\right\rvert
\lesssim \bigl(1-\vartheta(y)\bigr).
\]
The derivative vanishes for \(y<-1\); on \(-1<y<1\), this follows
from the boundedness of \(\vartheta'\), \(\vartheta''\), and the lower
bound \(1-\vartheta\ge e^{-1/4}\).  Since
\[
\nabla_\xi y=2\xi,
\qquad
t\partial_ty=\frac12a_\nu(t)^2,
\]
and a cutoff derivative can occur only for \(\left\lvert y\right\rvert<1\), where
\(a_\nu(t)^2\le1+s\), we obtain, almost everywhere on \(y\le1\),
\begin{equation}\label{eq:cutoff-factor-derivative-bounds}
\begin{aligned}
r\left\lvert \nabla_\xi(1-\widetilde\vartheta(y))\right\rvert
&\lesssim
s\bigl(1-\vartheta(y)\bigr),
\\
\left\lvert t\partial_t(1-\widetilde\vartheta(y))\right\rvert
&\lesssim
(1+s)\bigl(1-\vartheta(y)\bigr).
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}

The quadratic strain factor satisfies
\begin{equation}\label{eq:quadratic-strain-factor-bounds}
\begin{aligned}
\left\lvert \xi\cdot\Sigma(t)\xi^\perp\right\rvert
+r\left\lvert \nabla(\xi\cdot\Sigma(t)\xi^\perp)\right\rvert
+\left\lvert t\partial_t(\xi\cdot\Sigma(t)\xi^\perp)\right\rvert
\stackrel{\eqref{eq:Sigma-time-bound}}{\mathrel{\underset{T}{\lesssim}}} s
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Since \(\mathsf{p}_0=e^{s/4}(1-\vartheta(y))\) on \(y\le1\),
\eqref{eq:q-compact-form}, \eqref{eq:h-growth-bounds}, and
\eqref{eq:quadratic-strain-factor-bounds} imply
\[
\left\lvert q\right\rvert
\mathrel{\underset{T}{\lesssim}}
s(1+s)\mathsf{p}_0.
\]
Differentiating \eqref{eq:q-compact-form} on each smooth piece and
applying \eqref{eq:h-growth-bounds},
\eqref{eq:cutoff-factor-derivative-bounds}, and
\eqref{eq:quadratic-strain-factor-bounds} gives
\[
r\left\lvert \nabla q\right\rvert
+
\left\lvert t\partial_tq\right\rvert
\mathrel{\underset{T}{\lesssim}}
s(1+s)^2\mathsf{p}_0.
\]
On \(y>1\), \(q\) and its weak first derivatives vanish almost
everywhere, proving \eqref{eq:q-pointwise} and~\eqref{eq:q-derivative-pointwise}.

\smallskip
\noindent\emph{Estimates in the inner region.}
In the inner region \(s\le a_\nu(t)^2+1\), the bound
\(a_\nu(t)\ge2\) gives
\[
1+s
\le
a_\nu(t)^2+2
\le
\frac32a_\nu(t)^2.
\]
Since
\((\nu t)a_\nu(t)^4
\stackrel{\eqref{eq:weight-scales}}{=}a_0^4\), it follows that
\begin{equation}\label{eq:inner-weight-scale}
(\nu t)(1+s)^2
\le
\frac94a_0^4
\qquad\text{if }s\le a_\nu(t)^2+1.
\end{equation}

\[
(\nu t)\frac{\left\lvert q\right\rvert}{\mathsf{p}_0}
\stackrel{\eqref{eq:q-pointwise}}{\mathrel{\underset{T}{\lesssim}}}
(\nu t)s(1+s)
\stackrel{\eqref{eq:inner-weight-scale}}{\mathrel{\underset{T}{\lesssim}}}
a_0^4.
\]
This proves the first estimate in
\eqref{eq:normalized-q-bounds}; outside this region, \(q=0\).

For the second estimate, note first that
\[
B_\nu(t)
\stackrel{\eqref{eq:weight-scale-identity}}{=}
\frac{a_\nu(t)^2}{\beta}
\stackrel{\eqref{eq:standing-smallness}}{\ge}a_\nu(t)^2
>
\sqrt{a_\nu(t)^2+1},
\]
where \(a_\nu(t)\ge2\).  Thus this region is contained in the first two regions in the
definition of \(\chi_\nu\).  If \(r\le a_\nu(t)\), then
\[
\chi_\nu(t,\xi)
\stackrel{\eqref{eq:chi-def}}{=}
r^2.
\]
In the remaining part of the inner region,
\[
a_\nu(t)\le r\le\sqrt{a_\nu(t)^2+1},
\]
we have
$
\chi_\nu(t,\xi)
\stackrel{\eqref{eq:chi-def}}{=}
a_\nu(t)^2.
$
Since \(a_\nu(t)\ge2\),
\[
r^2
\le
a_\nu(t)^2+1
\le
\frac54a_\nu(t)^2
=
\frac54\chi_\nu(t,\xi).
\]
Thus \(s\le\frac54\chi_\nu(t,\xi)\) throughout the inner region.

By \eqref{eq:q-derivative-pointwise}, \eqref{eq:q-pointwise}, and
\eqref{eq:inner-weight-scale}, almost everywhere,
\[
(\nu t)
\frac{
r\left\lvert \nabla q\right\rvert
+\left\lvert q+t\partial_tq\right\rvert
}{\mathsf{p}_0}
\mathrel{\underset{T}{\lesssim}}
a_0^4\chi_\nu(t,\xi).
\]
This proves the second estimate in
\eqref{eq:normalized-q-bounds}.

$
\mathsf{p}
\stackrel{\eqref{eq:p-def}}{=}
\mathsf{p}_0+(\nu t)q,
$
and the first normalized estimate gives
\[
\left|
\frac{\mathsf{p}-\mathsf{p}_0}{\mathsf{p}_0}
\right|
=
(\nu t)\frac{\left\lvert q\right\rvert}{\mathsf{p}_0}
\stackrel{\eqref{eq:normalized-q-bounds}}{\mathrel{\underset{T}{\lesssim}}}
a_0^4.
\]
After decreasing \(a_0\), if necessary, so that the last bound is
at most \(1/4\), we conclude that
\[
\frac34\mathsf{p}_0
\le
\mathsf{p}
\le
\frac54\mathsf{p}_0.
\]
This proves \eqref{eq:p-comparable}.

\smallskip
\noindent\emph{Global upper and lower bounds.}
We first establish a lower bound for \(\mathsf{p}_0\). In the inner and
transition regions,
\[
\mathsf{p}_0
\stackrel{\eqref{eq:p0-vs-gaussian}}{\ge}
e^{-1/4}e^{r^2/4}.
\]
Moreover, since \(\beta\le1\),
\[
r^2-\beta r
=
\left(r-\frac{\beta}{2}\right)^2-\frac{\beta^2}{4}
\ge
-\frac14.
\]
Therefore,
\[
e^{-1/4}e^{r^2/4}
=
e^{-1/4}e^{\beta r/4}
e^{(r^2-\beta r)/4}
\ge
e^{-5/16}e^{\beta r/4}.
\]
Thus
\[
\mathsf{p}_0
\ge
e^{-5/16}e^{\beta r/4}
\]
in the inner and transition regions.

On the plateau, \(r\le B_\nu(t)\), and hence
\[
\beta r
\le
\beta B_\nu(t)
\stackrel{\eqref{eq:weight-scale-identity}}{=}
a_\nu(t)^2.
\]
Consequently,
\[
\mathsf{p}_0
=
e^{a_\nu(t)^2/4}
\ge
e^{\beta r/4}.
\]
In the outer region,
\[
\mathsf{p}_0
\stackrel{\eqref{eq:p0-def}}{=}
e^{\beta r/4}
\]
and therefore we have proved the global lower bound
\begin{equation}\label{eq:p0-global-lower}
\mathsf{p}_0(t,\xi)
\ge
e^{-5/16}e^{\beta r/4}.
\end{equation}

We next prove the global upper bound
\begin{equation}\label{eq:p0-below-gaussian}
\mathsf{p}_0(t,\xi)
\le
\mathsf{p}_G(\xi):=e^{r^2/4}.
\end{equation}
In the inner and transition regions,
\(\mathsf{p}_0\stackrel{\eqref{eq:p0-def}}{\le}e^{r^2/4}\).
On the plateau,
\[
\mathsf{p}_0
\stackrel{\eqref{eq:p0-def}}{=}
e^{a_\nu(t)^2/4}\le e^{r^2/4},
\]
because \(r^2\ge a_\nu(t)^2+1\). In the outer region,
\[
\mathsf{p}_0
\stackrel{\eqref{eq:p0-def}}{=}
e^{\beta r/4}\le e^{r^2/4},
\]
because \(r\ge B_\nu(t)>1\) and \(\beta\le1\), so that
\(\beta r\le r^2\). This proves
\eqref{eq:p0-below-gaussian}.

Finally,
\[
\mathsf{p}
\stackrel{\eqref{eq:p-comparable}}{\ge}
\frac34\mathsf{p}_0
\stackrel{\eqref{eq:p0-global-lower}}{\ge}
\frac34e^{-5/16}e^{\beta r/4}
\ge
\frac12e^{\beta r/4},
\]
because \((3/4)e^{-5/16}>1/2\), and
\[
\mathsf{p}
\stackrel{\eqref{eq:p-comparable}}{\le}
\frac54\mathsf{p}_0
\stackrel{\eqref{eq:p0-below-gaussian}}{\le}
\frac54e^{r^2/4}
\le
2e^{r^2/4}.
\]
Therefore,
\[
\frac12e^{\frac{\beta\left\lvert \xi\right\rvert}{4}}
\le
\mathsf{p}(t,\xi)
\le
2e^{\frac{\left\lvert \xi\right\rvert^2}{4}}.
\]
This proves \eqref{eq:p-global-bounds} and completes the proof.
<!-- end proof-28 -->
<!-- lemma-24: body_md -->
\label{lem:energy-id}
Let \(w\) be a smooth solution of \eqref{eq:linearized}, let
\(v=K\star_\xi w\), and let \(\mathsf{p}=\mathsf{p}(t,\xi)\) be a positive,
continuous, locally Lipschitz weight with the integrability required
below. The first weak derivatives of \(\mathsf{p}\) agree almost everywhere
with its derivatives on the smooth pieces, and continuity at the
transition circles cancels the interface terms. Then, for almost every
time,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:energy-id}
\begin{aligned}
t\frac{d}{dt}E_{\mathsf{p}}(t)
\stackrel{\substack{\eqref{eq:linearized}\\
\eqref{eq:energy}}}{=}&
\frac{t}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}(\partial_t\mathsf{p})\left\lvert w\right\rvert^2\,d\xi
-
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\mathsf{p}\left\lvert \nabla w\right\rvert^2\,d\xi
-
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}w\nabla\mathsf{p}\cdot\nabla w\,d\xi
\\
&-
\frac14\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}(\xi\cdot\nabla\mathsf{p})\left\lvert w\right\rvert^2\,d\xi
-
\frac12\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\mathsf{p}\left\lvert w\right\rvert^2\,d\xi
\\
&+
\frac{1}{2\nu}
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}(v^G\cdot\nabla\mathsf{p})\left\lvert w\right\rvert^2\,d\xi
+
\frac12\sqrt{\frac{t}{\nu}}
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}(b\cdot\nabla\mathsf{p})\left\lvert w\right\rvert^2\,d\xi
\\
&-
\frac1\nu
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\mathsf{p}(v\cdot\nabla G)w\,d\xi
+
\left\langle f(t),w(t)\right\rangle_{\mathsf{p}(t)}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
<!-- end lemma-24 -->
<!-- proof-29: body_md -->
Since \(\mathcal{L} w-w=\Delta w+\frac12\xi\cdot\nabla w\),
integration by parts gives
\[
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\mathsf{p} w\Delta w\,d\xi
=
-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\mathsf{p}\left\lvert \nabla w\right\rvert^2\,d\xi
-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}w\nabla\mathsf{p}\cdot\nabla w\,d\xi
\]
and
\[
\begin{aligned}
\frac12\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\mathsf{p} w\,\xi\cdot\nabla w\,d\xi
&=
\frac14\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\mathsf{p}\,\xi\cdot\nabla\left\lvert w\right\rvert^2\,d\xi
\\
&=
-\frac12\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\mathsf{p}\left\lvert w\right\rvert^2\,d\xi
-\frac14\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}(\xi\cdot\nabla\mathsf{p})\left\lvert w\right\rvert^2\,d\xi.
\end{aligned}
\]
Moreover,
\(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\mathsf{p} w\,t\partial_tw\,d\xi
=t\,dE_{\mathsf{p}}/dt-(t/2)\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}(\partial_t\mathsf{p})\left\lvert w\right\rvert^2\,d\xi\).
Finally, \(v^G\) and \(b\) are divergence free, and therefore
\[
\begin{aligned}
-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\mathsf{p} w\,(v^G\cdot\nabla w)\,d\xi
&=\frac12\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}(v^G\cdot\nabla\mathsf{p})\left\lvert w\right\rvert^2\,d\xi,
\\
-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\mathsf{p} w\,(b\cdot\nabla w)\,d\xi
&=\frac12\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}(b\cdot\nabla\mathsf{p})\left\lvert w\right\rvert^2\,d\xi.
\end{aligned}
\]
These identities establish \eqref{eq:energy-id}.
For a piecewise \(C^1\) weight, one may perform the calculation in each
smooth region; the interface contributions cancel because \(\mathsf{p}\) is
continuous.  Equivalently, approximate \(\mathsf{p}\) by smooth positive weights
and pass to the limit.
<!-- end proof-29 -->
<!-- theorem-4: body_md -->
\label{thm:weight-coercivity}
Recall \(\mathcal D_{\mathsf{p}}(t)\) from
\eqref{eq:energy-parts-def}, \(\mathcal I(t),\mathcal J(t),\mathcal K(t)\)
from \eqref{eq:IJK-def}, and \(\mathcal Q(t)\) from
\eqref{eq:Q-def}.  For \(a_0\) sufficiently small, depending only on
\(T\), and under \eqref{eq:standing-smallness},
\begin{equation}\label{eq:weight-coercivity}
\mathcal D_{\mathsf{p}}(t)
\le
-\frac3{16}\mathcal I(t)
-\frac1{128}\mathcal J(t)
-\frac38\mathcal K(t)
\le
-\frac1{128}\mathcal Q(t).
\end{equation}
<!-- end theorem-4 -->
<!-- proof-30: body_md -->
Expanding the angular part of the weight gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:diffusive-full-splitting}
\begin{aligned}
\mathcal D_{\mathsf{p}}(t)
&\stackrel{\substack{\eqref{eq:energy-parts-def}\\
\eqref{eq:p-def}}}{=}
\frac{t}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}(\partial_t\mathsf{p}_0)|w|^2\,d\xi
-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\mathsf{p}_0|\nabla w|^2\,d\xi
-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}w\nabla\mathsf{p}_0\cdot\nabla w\,d\xi
\\
&-
\frac14\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}(\xi\cdot\nabla\mathsf{p}_0)|w|^2\,d\xi
-\frac12\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\mathsf{p}_0|w|^2\,d\xi
\\
&+
\frac{\nu t}{2}
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}(q+t\partial_tq)|w|^2\,d\xi
-(\nu t)\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}q|\nabla w|^2\,d\xi
\\
&-
(\nu t)\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}w\nabla q\cdot\nabla w\,d\xi
-\frac{\nu t}{4}
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}(\xi\cdot\nabla q)|w|^2\,d\xi
\\
&-
\frac{\nu t}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}q|w|^2\,d\xi .
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}

We first estimate the contribution of the background weight
\(\mathsf{p}_0\). Let
\[
\begin{aligned}
\mathcal D_{\mathsf{p}_0}
:={}&
\frac{t}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}(\partial_t\mathsf{p}_0)|w|^2\,d\xi
-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\mathsf{p}_0|\nabla w|^2\,d\xi
-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}w\nabla\mathsf{p}_0\cdot\nabla w\,d\xi
\\
&-
\frac14\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}(\xi\cdot\nabla\mathsf{p}_0)|w|^2\,d\xi
-\frac12\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\mathsf{p}_0|w|^2\,d\xi .
\end{aligned}
\]

The mixed term satisfies, by Young's inequality,
\[
\begin{aligned}
\left|
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}w\nabla\mathsf{p}_0\cdot\nabla w\,d\xi
\right|
&\le
\frac34
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\mathsf{p}_0|\nabla w|^2\,d\xi
\\
&\quad+
\frac13
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}
\frac{|\nabla\mathsf{p}_0|^2}{\mathsf{p}_0}|w|^2\,d\xi .
\end{aligned}
\]
Therefore,
\[
\begin{aligned}
\mathcal D_{\mathsf{p}_0}
\le{}&
-\frac14
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\mathsf{p}_0|\nabla w|^2\,d\xi
\\
&+
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}
\left(
\frac t2\partial_t\mathsf{p}_0
-\frac14\xi\cdot\nabla\mathsf{p}_0
-\frac12\mathsf{p}_0
+\frac13\frac{|\nabla\mathsf{p}_0|^2}{\mathsf{p}_0}
\right)
|w|^2\,d\xi .
\end{aligned}
\]

The coefficient of \(|w|^2\) can be written as
\[
\begin{aligned}
&
\frac t2\partial_t\mathsf{p}_0
-\frac14\xi\cdot\nabla\mathsf{p}_0
-\frac12\mathsf{p}_0
+\frac13\frac{|\nabla\mathsf{p}_0|^2}{\mathsf{p}_0}
\\
&=
-\mathsf{p}_0
\left(
\frac{\xi\cdot\nabla\mathsf{p}_0}{4\mathsf{p}_0}
-\frac{|\nabla\mathsf{p}_0|^2}{3\mathsf{p}_0^2}
-\frac{t\partial_t\mathsf{p}_0}{2\mathsf{p}_0}
+\frac12
\right).
\end{aligned}
\]

The pointwise coercivity estimate gives
\[
\frac{\xi\cdot\nabla\mathsf{p}_0}{4\mathsf{p}_0}
-\frac{|\nabla\mathsf{p}_0|^2}{3\mathsf{p}_0^2}
-\frac{t\partial_t\mathsf{p}_0}{2\mathsf{p}_0}
\stackrel{\eqref{eq:p0-pointwise-coercivity}}{\ge}
\frac1{48}\chi_\nu(t,\xi).
\]
Consequently,
\[
\begin{aligned}
\mathcal D_{\mathsf{p}_0}
\le{}&
-\frac14
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\mathsf{p}_0|\nabla w|^2\,d\xi
\\
&-
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}
\mathsf{p}_0
\left(
\frac1{48}\chi_\nu(t,\xi)+\frac12
\right)
|w|^2\,d\xi .
\end{aligned}
\]
\begin{equation}\label{eq:p0-part-coercivity}
\mathcal D_{\mathsf{p}_0}
\stackrel{\eqref{eq:IJK-def}}{\le}
-\frac14\mathcal I(t)
-\frac1{48}\mathcal J(t)
-\frac12\mathcal K(t).
\end{equation}

It remains to control the five terms in
\eqref{eq:diffusive-full-splitting} that contain \(q\).  Denote their
sum by \(\mathcal D_q(t)\), so that
\(\mathcal D_{\mathsf{p}}(t)=\mathcal D_{\mathsf{p}_0}(t)+\mathcal D_q(t)\).
Choose \(c_{q,T}>0\), independent of \(a_0,B_0,\nu\),
large enough that the three estimates below hold, and set
\(\delta_T:=c_{q,T}a_0^4\).
Then, for every \(0<t\le T\) and almost every \(\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\),
\begin{equation}\label{eq:q-diffusive-normalized-bounds}
\begin{aligned}
(\nu t)\left\lvert q\right\rvert
&\stackrel{\eqref{eq:normalized-q-bounds}}{\le}
\delta_T\mathsf{p}_0,
\\
(\nu t)
\bigl(
  \left\lvert q+t\partial_tq\right\rvert
  +\left\lvert \xi\right\rvert\left\lvert \nabla q\right\rvert
\bigr)
&\stackrel{\eqref{eq:normalized-q-bounds}}{\le}
\delta_T\mathsf{p}_0\chi_\nu,
\\
(\nu t)\left\lvert \nabla q\right\rvert
&\stackrel{\substack{\eqref{eq:normalized-q-bounds}\\
\eqref{eq:chi-def}}}{\le}
\delta_T\mathsf{p}_0\chi_\nu^{1/2}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
For the third line, in the inner region \(\left\lvert \xi\right\rvert^2\le a_\nu(t)^2+1\),
\(a_\nu(t)\ge2\) implies
\(\chi_\nu(t,\xi)\stackrel{\eqref{eq:chi-def}}{\simeq}\left\lvert \xi\right\rvert^2\).
Thus \(\chi_\nu/\left\lvert \xi\right\rvert\lesssim \chi_\nu^{1/2}\) for \(\xi\ne0\);
outside this region, the weak derivatives of \(q\) vanish almost everywhere.

\begin{equation}\label{eq:q-gradient-coefficient-bound}
\left|
(\nu t)\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}q\left\lvert \nabla w\right\rvert^2\,d\xi
\right|
\stackrel{\eqref{eq:q-diffusive-normalized-bounds}}{\le}
\delta_T\mathcal I(t).
\end{equation}
For the mixed term, Young's inequality gives
\begin{align}
\left|
(\nu t)\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}w\nabla q\cdot\nabla w\,d\xi
\right|
&\stackrel{\eqref{eq:q-diffusive-normalized-bounds}}{\le}
\delta_T
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}
\mathsf{p}_0\chi_\nu^{1/2}\left\lvert w\right\rvert\left\lvert \nabla w\right\rvert\,d\xi
\le
\frac1{32}\mathcal I(t)
+8\delta_T^2\mathcal J(t).
\label{eq:q-mixed-diffusive-bound}
\end{align}
Finally, the zeroth-order terms satisfy
\begin{align}
&\left|
\frac{\nu t}{2}
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}(q+t\partial_tq)\left\lvert w\right\rvert^2\,d\xi
\right|
+
\left|
\frac{\nu t}{4}
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}(\xi\cdot\nabla q)\left\lvert w\right\rvert^2\,d\xi
\right|
\stackrel{\eqref{eq:q-diffusive-normalized-bounds}}{\le}
\frac34\delta_T\mathcal J(t),
\label{eq:q-confinement-terms-bound}
\\
&\left|
\frac{\nu t}{2}
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}q\left\lvert w\right\rvert^2\,d\xi
\right|
\stackrel{\eqref{eq:q-diffusive-normalized-bounds}}{\le}
\frac12\delta_T\mathcal K(t).
\label{eq:q-L2-term-bound}
\end{align}
Combining \eqref{eq:q-gradient-coefficient-bound},
\eqref{eq:q-mixed-diffusive-bound},
\eqref{eq:q-confinement-terms-bound}, and \eqref{eq:q-L2-term-bound},
we bound the sum of the five terms by
\begin{equation}\label{eq:q-part-coercivity-error}
\mathcal D_q(t)
\le
\left(\delta_T+\frac1{32}\right)\mathcal I(t)
+\left(\frac34\delta_T+8\delta_T^2\right)\mathcal J(t)
+\frac12\delta_T\mathcal K(t).
\end{equation}
Since \(\delta_T=c_{q,T}a_0^4\), taking \(a_0\) small enough, depending
only on \(T\), gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:q-part-absorbed}
\mathcal D_q(t)
\stackrel{\eqref{eq:q-part-coercivity-error}}{\le}
\frac1{16}\mathcal I(t)
+\frac5{384}\mathcal J(t)
+\frac18\mathcal K(t).
\end{equation}
Since \(1/48-5/384=1/128\) and
\(\mathcal I,\mathcal J,\mathcal K\ge0\),
\[
\begin{aligned}
\mathcal D_{\mathsf{p}}
&\stackrel{\substack{\eqref{eq:p0-part-coercivity}\\
\eqref{eq:q-part-absorbed}}}{\le}
-\frac3{16}\mathcal I-\frac1{128}\mathcal J-\frac38\mathcal K
\\
&\stackrel{\eqref{eq:Q-def}}{\le}-\frac1{128}\mathcal Q.
\end{aligned}
\]
This proves \eqref{eq:weight-coercivity}.
<!-- end proof-30 -->
<!-- lemma-25: body_md -->
\label{lem:transport-estimates}
For the weight \eqref{eq:p-def}, there exists
\(c_{\mathrm{tr},T}\ge1\), independent of \(a_0,B_0,\nu\)
in the ranges considered above, such that
\begin{equation}\label{eq:transport-total-bound}
\left\lvert \mathcal T_{\mathsf{p}}(t)\right\rvert
\le
c_{\mathrm{tr},T}
\left(
a_0(\nu T)^{\frac14}
+a_0^4
+\frac1{B_0}
\right)\mathcal J(t),
\qquad 0<t\le T.
\end{equation}
<!-- end lemma-25 -->
<!-- proof-31: body_md -->
Fix \(t\in(0,T]\), and suppress the time dependence of the
integration domains. Since \(\mathsf{p}_0\) is radial,
\(v^G\cdot\nabla\mathsf{p}_0=0\). Moreover,
\(q=\nabla q=0\) almost everywhere outside
\(\Omega_{\mathrm{in}}\), and
\(\nabla\mathsf{p}_0=0\) on \(\Omega_{\mathrm{mid}}\).
Thus \eqref{eq:energy-parts-def}, \eqref{eq:p-def}, and
\eqref{eq:q-cell} give
\[
\begin{aligned}
\mathcal T_{\mathsf{p}}(t)
={}&
\frac12\sqrt{\frac t\nu}
\int_{\Omega_{\mathrm{in}}}
\bigl(b-\sqrt{\nu t}\,\Sigma(t)\xi\bigr)
\cdot\nabla\mathsf{p}_0\,\left\lvert w\right\rvert^2\,d\xi
\\
&+
\frac{\nu t}{2}\sqrt{\frac t\nu}
\int_{\Omega_{\mathrm{in}}}
(b\cdot\nabla q)\left\lvert w\right\rvert^2\,d\xi
\\
&+
\frac12\sqrt{\frac t\nu}
\int_{\Omega_{\mathrm{out}}}
(b\cdot\nabla\mathsf{p}_0)\left\lvert w\right\rvert^2\,d\xi
\\
=:{}&
\mathcal T_1+\mathcal T_2+\mathcal T_3.
\end{aligned}
\]
In particular, the leading strain contribution cancels exactly.

~\\
By \eqref{eq:p0-gradient} and \eqref{eq:rho-b-def},
\[
\mathcal T_1
=
\frac14\sqrt{\frac t\nu}
\int_{\Omega_{\mathrm{in}}}
\rho_b e^{\frac{\left\lvert \xi\right\rvert^2}{4}}
\left(
1-\widetilde\vartheta
\bigl(\left\lvert \xi\right\rvert^2-a_\nu(t)^2\bigr)
\right)\left\lvert w\right\rvert^2\,d\xi.
\]
Since
\(0\le1-\widetilde\vartheta\le1-\vartheta\),
\eqref{eq:p0-def} and \eqref{eq:rho-b-bound} imply
\[
\left\lvert \mathcal T_1\right\rvert
\mathrel{\underset{T}{\lesssim}}
t\sqrt{\nu t}
\int_{\Omega_{\mathrm{in}}}
\left\lvert \xi\right\rvert^3\mathsf{p}_0\left\lvert w\right\rvert^2\,d\xi.
\]
The standing assumption \eqref{eq:standing-smallness} gives
\(a_\nu(t)\ge2\). Hence, by \eqref{eq:chi-def},
\[
\left\lvert \xi\right\rvert\le2a_\nu(t),
\qquad
\left\lvert \xi\right\rvert^2\le\frac54\chi_\nu(t,\xi),
\qquad \xi\in\Omega_{\mathrm{in}},
\]
and therefore
\[
\begin{aligned}
\left\lvert \mathcal T_1\right\rvert
&\mathrel{\underset{T}{\lesssim}}
t\sqrt{\nu t}\,a_\nu(t)
\int_{\Omega_{\mathrm{in}}}
\mathsf{p}_0\chi_\nu\left\lvert w\right\rvert^2\,d\xi
\le
C_T t a_0(\nu t)^{\frac14}\mathcal J(t),
\end{aligned}
\]
where we used \eqref{eq:weight-scales} and
\eqref{eq:IJK-def}.

~\\
For the second term, the local velocity bound in
\eqref{eq:linear-b-bounds} gives
\[
\sqrt{\frac t\nu}\left\lvert b(\xi,t)\right\rvert
\mathrel{\underset{T}{\lesssim}}t\left\lvert \xi\right\rvert,
\qquad \xi\in\Omega_{\mathrm{in}}.
\]
Using \eqref{eq:normalized-q-bounds}, we obtain
\[
\begin{aligned}
\left\lvert \mathcal T_2\right\rvert
&\mathrel{\underset{T}{\lesssim}}
t(\nu t)
\int_{\Omega_{\mathrm{in}}}
\left\lvert \xi\right\rvert\left\lvert \nabla q\right\rvert\left\lvert w\right\rvert^2\,d\xi
\\
&\mathrel{\underset{T}{\lesssim}}
t a_0^4
\int_{\Omega_{\mathrm{in}}}
\mathsf{p}_0\chi_\nu\left\lvert w\right\rvert^2\,d\xi
\\
&\le C_T t a_0^4\mathcal J(t).
\end{aligned}
\]

~\\
Finally, on \(\Omega_{\mathrm{out}}\),
\eqref{eq:linear-b-bounds}, \eqref{eq:p0-gradient}, and
\eqref{eq:chi-def} give
\[
\left\lvert b\right\rvert\mathrel{\underset{T}{\lesssim}}1,
\qquad
\left\lvert \nabla\mathsf{p}_0\right\rvert=\frac{\beta}{4}\mathsf{p}_0,
\qquad
\chi_\nu=\beta\left\lvert \xi\right\rvert.
\]
Since
\(\left\lvert \xi\right\rvert\ge B_\nu(t)=B_0(\nu t)^{-1/2}\), we also have
\[
\sqrt{\frac t\nu}\le\frac{t\left\lvert \xi\right\rvert}{B_0}.
\]
Consequently,
\[
\begin{aligned}
\left\lvert \mathcal T_3\right\rvert
&\mathrel{\underset{T}{\lesssim}}
\frac{t}{B_0}
\int_{\Omega_{\mathrm{out}}}
\beta\left\lvert \xi\right\rvert\,\mathsf{p}_0\left\lvert w\right\rvert^2\,d\xi
\le
\frac{C_Tt}{B_0}\mathcal J(t).
\end{aligned}
\]
Summing the three estimates and using \(t\le T\) proves
\eqref{eq:transport-total-bound}, with
\(c_{\mathrm{tr},T}\) independent of \(a_0,B_0,\nu\).
<!-- end proof-31 -->
<!-- lemma-26: body_md -->
\label{lem:basic-biotsavart}
For the fixed parameters \(a_0,B_0\), uniformly in \(\nu\),
\begin{equation}\label{eq:w-L43}
\left\lVert w\right\rVert_{L^{\frac{4}{3}}}
\mathrel{\underset{T,\beta}{\lesssim}}
\mathcal K(t)^{\frac{1}{2}},
\end{equation}
\begin{equation}\label{eq:v-L4}
\left\lVert v\right\rVert_{L^4}
\mathrel{\underset{T,\beta}{\lesssim}}
\mathcal K(t)^{\frac{1}{2}},
\end{equation}
and
\begin{equation}\label{eq:w-L4}
\left\lVert w\right\rVert_{L^4}
\lesssim
\mathcal K(t)^{\frac{1}{4}}\mathcal I(t)^{\frac{1}{4}}.
\end{equation}
Consequently,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:v-Linfty-basic}
\left\lVert v\right\rVert_{L^\infty}
\mathrel{\underset{T,\beta}{\lesssim}}
\mathcal K(t)^{\frac{1}{4}}
\bigl(\mathcal I(t)+\mathcal K(t)\bigr)^{\frac{1}{4}}.
\end{equation}
<!-- end lemma-26 -->
<!-- proof-32: body_md -->
The weight satisfies
\(\mathsf{p}_0^{-1/2}\stackrel{\eqref{eq:p0-global-lower}}{\lesssim}
e^{-\beta\left\lvert \xi\right\rvert/8}\in L^4(\mathbb{R}^{2})\), where the
norm depends only on the fixed \(\beta>0\).  H\"older's inequality and
the Hardy--Littlewood--Sobolev inequality therefore give
\[
\left\lVert w\right\rVert_{L^{4/3}}
\stackrel{\substack{\eqref{eq:p-comparable}\\
\eqref{eq:p-global-bounds}}}{\mathrel{\underset{\beta}{\lesssim}}}
\mathcal K^{1/2},
\qquad
\left\lVert v\right\rVert_{L^4}
\lesssim\left\lVert w\right\rVert_{L^{4/3}}.
\]
Since \(\mathsf{p}_0\ge1\), the two-dimensional Gagliardo--Nirenberg inequality
also gives
\[
\left\lVert w\right\rVert_{L^4}\lesssim
\left\lVert w\right\rVert_{L^2}^{1/2}\left\lVert \nabla w\right\rVert_{L^2}^{1/2}
\le\mathcal K^{1/4}\mathcal I^{1/4}.
\]
Finally, splitting the Biot--Savart integral into
\(\{\left\lvert \xi-\eta\right\rvert<R\}\) and its complement yields
\[
\left\lVert v\right\rVert_{L^\infty}
\lesssim
\left(
R^{1/2}\left\lVert w\right\rVert_{L^4}
+
R^{-1/2}\left\lVert w\right\rVert_{L^{4/3}}
\right).
\]
Choosing \(R=\left\lVert w\right\rVert_{L^{4/3}}/\left\lVert w\right\rVert_{L^4}\), with the zero cases
understood by approximation, gives
\[
\begin{aligned}
\left\lVert v\right\rVert_{L^\infty}
&\lesssim \left\lVert w\right\rVert_{L^4}^{1/2}\left\lVert w\right\rVert_{L^{4/3}}^{1/2}
\\
&\stackrel{\substack{\eqref{eq:w-L4}\\
\eqref{eq:w-L43}}}{\mathrel{\underset{T,\beta}{\lesssim}}}
\mathcal K^{3/8}\mathcal I^{1/8}
\mathrel{\underset{T,\beta}{\lesssim}} \mathcal K^{1/4}(\mathcal I+\mathcal K)^{1/4}.
\end{aligned}
\]
This proves \eqref{eq:v-Linfty-basic}.
<!-- end proof-32 -->
<!-- lemma-27: body_md -->
\label{lem:v-decay}
Assume \(w\in H^1(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathsf{p}\,d\xi)\) and \(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}w(\xi)\,d\xi=0\).
Then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:v-decay}
\left\lvert v(\xi)\right\rvert
\mathrel{\underset{\beta}{\lesssim}}
\frac{1}{1+\left\lvert \xi\right\rvert^2}
\left(
\left\lVert w\right\rVert_{L^2_{\mathsf{p}}}
+
\left\lVert \nabla w\right\rVert_{L^2_{\mathsf{p}}}
\right).
\end{equation}
The implicit constant is uniform in \(t\in(0,T]\) and
\(0<\nu\le\nu_T\), with the fixed \(\beta=\frac{a_0^2}{B_0}>0\).
<!-- end lemma-27 -->
<!-- proof-33: body_md -->
Set \(g(\xi)=(1+\left\lvert \xi\right\rvert^2)w(\xi)\). We first prove
\begin{equation}\label{eq:v-weighted-unweighted}
\left\lVert 
(1+\left\lvert \xi\right\rvert^2)v
\right\rVert_{L^\infty}
\lesssim
\left\lVert 
(1+\left\lvert \xi\right\rvert^2)w
\right\rVert_{L^4\cap L^{\frac{4}{3}}}.
\end{equation}
Write \(M=\left\lVert g\right\rVert_{L^4}+\left\lVert g\right\rVert_{L^{4/3}}\).
Splitting the kernel at unit distance and applying H\"older's
inequality gives
\[
\sup_{\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\left\lvert K(\xi-\eta)\right\rvert\left\lvert g(\eta)\right\rvert\,d\eta
\lesssim M,
\qquad
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\left\lvert \eta\right\rvert\left\lvert w(\eta)\right\rvert\,d\eta\lesssim M,
\]
where the second estimate uses
\(\left\lvert \eta\right\rvert/(1+\left\lvert \eta\right\rvert^2)\in L^4(\mathbb{R}^{2})\).
The first estimate controls \(v(\xi)\) when \(\left\lvert \xi\right\rvert\le2\).
For \(R=\left\lvert \xi\right\rvert\ge2\), the zero-mass condition yields
\[
\begin{aligned}
v(\xi)
={}&\int_{\left\lvert \eta\right\rvert\le R/2}
\bigl(K(\xi-\eta)-K(\xi)\bigr)w(\eta)\,d\eta
\\
&+\int_{\left\lvert \eta\right\rvert>R/2}K(\xi-\eta)w(\eta)\,d\eta
-K(\xi)\int_{\left\lvert \eta\right\rvert>R/2}w(\eta)\,d\eta.
\end{aligned}
\]
On the first region,
\(\left\lvert K(\xi-\eta)-K(\xi)\right\rvert\lesssim R^{-2}\left\lvert \eta\right\rvert\).
On the second region, \(\left\lvert w(\eta)\right\rvert\le4R^{-2}\left\lvert g(\eta)\right\rvert\).
The last term is bounded by
\(CR^{-2}\int\left\lvert \eta\right\rvert\left\lvert w(\eta)\right\rvert\,d\eta\).
Thus all three terms are bounded by \(CR^{-2}M\), proving
\eqref{eq:v-weighted-unweighted}.

~\\
Set \(W=(1+\left\lvert \xi\right\rvert^2)\mathsf{p}^{-1/2}\). The weight satisfies
\(W\stackrel{\eqref{eq:p-global-bounds}}{\le}
\sqrt2(1+\left\lvert \xi\right\rvert^2)e^{-\beta\left\lvert \xi\right\rvert/8}
\in L^4\cap L^\infty\).  Hence
\[
\begin{aligned}
\left\lVert g\right\rVert_{L^{4/3}}
&\le\left\lVert W\right\rVert_{L^4}\left\lVert w\right\rVert_{L^2_{\mathsf{p}}},\\
\left\lVert g\right\rVert_{L^2}
&\le\left\lVert W\right\rVert_{L^\infty}\left\lVert w\right\rVert_{L^2_{\mathsf{p}}}.
\end{aligned}
\]
Moreover, since
\(\nabla g=2\xi w+(1+\left\lvert \xi\right\rvert^2)\nabla w\),
\[
\left\lVert \nabla g\right\rVert_{L^2}
\lesssim
\left\lVert W\right\rVert_{L^\infty}
\left(
\left\lVert w\right\rVert_{L^2_{\mathsf{p}}}
+
\left\lVert \nabla w\right\rVert_{L^2_{\mathsf{p}}}
\right).
\]
The two-dimensional Gagliardo--Nirenberg inequality controls
\(\left\lVert g\right\rVert_{L^4}\).  Consequently,
\[
\begin{aligned}
\left\lVert (1+\left\lvert \xi\right\rvert^2)v\right\rVert_{L^\infty}
&\stackrel{\eqref{eq:v-weighted-unweighted}}{\lesssim}
\left\lVert g\right\rVert_{L^4\cap L^{4/3}}
\\
&\mathrel{\underset{\beta}{\lesssim}} 
\left(\left\lVert w\right\rVert_{L^2_{\mathsf{p}}}
+\left\lVert \nabla w\right\rVert_{L^2_{\mathsf{p}}}\right),
\end{aligned}
\]
which proves \eqref{eq:v-decay}.
<!-- end proof-33 -->
<!-- lemma-28: body_md -->
\label{lem:reaction-cancellation}
For every real-valued \(w\) such that
\((1+\left\lvert \xi\right\rvert)w\in L^1\cap L^2\), with \(v=K\star w\), \(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}(v\cdot\xi)w\,d\xi=0\).
Consequently,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:gaussian-reaction-zero}
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}
e^{\frac{\left\lvert \xi\right\rvert^2}{4}}
(v\cdot\nabla G)w\,d\xi
=0.
\end{equation}
<!-- end lemma-28 -->
<!-- proof-34: body_md -->
Symmetrizing the Biot--Savart integral gives
\[
\begin{aligned}
2\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}(v\cdot\xi)w\,d\xi
&=
\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}}
K(\xi-\eta)\cdot(\xi-\eta)
w(\xi)w(\eta)\,d\eta\,d\xi
\\
&=0,
\end{aligned}
\]
because \(K(z)\cdot z=0\). Since
\(\nabla G(\xi)=-(8\pi)^{-1}\xi e^{-\left\lvert \xi\right\rvert^2/4}\),
\eqref{eq:gaussian-reaction-zero} follows.
<!-- end proof-34 -->
<!-- lemma-29: body_md -->
\label{lem:reaction}
For the fixed parameters \(a_0,B_0\) and every \(\eta>0\),
there exists \(\nu_{T,\eta}>0\) such that
\begin{equation}\label{eq:reaction-bound}
\bigl|\mathcal R_{\mathsf p}(t)\bigr|
\le
\eta\mathcal K(t)
+C_{T,\beta}\,t\mathcal K(t),
\qquad
0<\nu\le\nu_{T,\eta},
\quad 0<t\le T,
\end{equation}
where \(C_{T,\beta}\) is independent of \(\nu,t,\eta\).
<!-- end lemma-29 -->
<!-- proof-35: body_md -->
Let \(\mathsf p_G(\xi)=e^{|\xi|^2/4}\).
By \eqref{eq:gaussian-reaction-zero} and \eqref{eq:p-def},
\[
\begin{aligned}
\mathcal R_{\mathsf p}(t)
={}&
-\frac1\nu
\int_{\mathbb R^2}
(\mathsf p_0-\mathsf p_G)(v\cdot\nabla G)w\,d\xi
\\
&-
t\int_{\mathbb R^2}q(v\cdot\nabla G)w\,d\xi.
\end{aligned}
\]
Thus H\"older's inequality and \eqref{eq:v-L4} give
\[
\begin{aligned}
\bigl|\mathcal R_{\mathsf p}(t)\bigr|
&\le
\|v\|_{L^4}
\|\mathsf p_0^{1/2}w\|_{L^2}
\left(
\frac1\nu
\left\|
\frac{(\mathsf p_0-\mathsf p_G)\nabla G}
{\mathsf p_0^{1/2}}
\right\|_{L^4}
+
t\left\|
\frac{q\nabla G}{\mathsf p_0^{1/2}}
\right\|_{L^4}
\right)
\\
&\le
C_{T,\beta}\mathcal K(t)
\left(
\frac1\nu
\left\|
\frac{(\mathsf p_0-\mathsf p_G)\nabla G}
{\mathsf p_0^{1/2}}
\right\|_{L^4}
+
t\left\|
\frac{q\nabla G}{\mathsf p_0^{1/2}}
\right\|_{L^4}
\right).
\end{aligned}
\]

~\\
We first estimate the coefficient containing
\(\mathsf p_0-\mathsf p_G\).
Set
\[
r_\nu:=\frac{a_0}{2}(\nu T)^{-1/4}.
\]
By \eqref{eq:standing-smallness} and \eqref{eq:weight-scales},
\(a_\nu(t)\ge2\) and
\[
\sqrt{a_\nu(t)^2-1}
\ge \frac12a_\nu(t)
\ge r_\nu,
\qquad 0<t\le T.
\]
Since \(\mathsf p_0=\mathsf p_G\) when
\(|\xi|^2\le a_\nu(t)^2-1\), the difference
\(\mathsf p_0-\mathsf p_G\) vanishes on \(\{|\xi|<r_\nu\}\).
Moreover, \eqref{eq:p0-below-gaussian} gives
\[
|\mathsf p_0-\mathsf p_G|\,|\nabla G|
\le \mathsf p_G|\nabla G|
\lesssim |\xi|.
\]
Using \eqref{eq:p0-global-lower}, we obtain
\[
\left|
\frac{(\mathsf p_0-\mathsf p_G)\nabla G}
{\mathsf p_0^{1/2}}
\right|
\lesssim
|\xi|e^{-\beta|\xi|/8}
\mathbf 1_{\{|\xi|\ge r_\nu\}}.
\]
For sufficiently small \(\nu\), one has \(\beta r_\nu\ge2\), and
polar coordinates yield
\[
\begin{aligned}
\left\|
|\xi|e^{-\beta|\xi|/8}
\mathbf 1_{\{|\xi|\ge r_\nu\}}
\right\|_{L^4}^4
&=
2\pi\int_{r_\nu}^{\infty}r^5e^{-\beta r/2}\,dr
\lesssim
\beta^{-1}r_\nu^5e^{-\beta r_\nu/2}.
\end{aligned}
\]
Consequently,
\[
\sup_{0<t\le T}
\frac1\nu
\left\|
\frac{(\mathsf p_0-\mathsf p_G)\nabla G}
{\mathsf p_0^{1/2}}
\right\|_{L^4}
\lesssim
\frac{\beta^{-1/4}}{\nu}
r_\nu^{5/4}e^{-\beta r_\nu/8}
\to0
\qquad\text{as }\nu\to0,
\]
because \(r_\nu=\frac{a_0}{2}(\nu T)^{-1/4}\).

~\\
For the remaining coefficient, \eqref{eq:q-pointwise} and
\eqref{eq:p0-below-gaussian} imply
\[
\begin{aligned}
\frac{|q\nabla G|}{\mathsf p_0^{1/2}}
&\le
C_T|\xi|^3(1+|\xi|^2)
\mathsf p_0^{1/2}e^{-|\xi|^2/4}
\\
&\le
C_T|\xi|^3(1+|\xi|^2)e^{-|\xi|^2/8}.
\end{aligned}
\]
The right-hand side belongs to \(L^4(\mathbb R^2)\), so
\[
\left\|
\frac{q\nabla G}{\mathsf p_0^{1/2}}
\right\|_{L^4}
\le C_T,
\]
uniformly in \(t\) and \(\nu\).
Returning to the initial estimate and choosing
\(\nu_{T,\eta}\) sufficiently small proves
\eqref{eq:reaction-bound}.
<!-- end proof-35 -->
<!-- proof-36: body_md -->
First choose \(0<a_0\ll1\) so that
Lemma~\ref{lem:adapted-weight} and
Theorem~\ref{thm:weight-coercivity} apply and
\(c_{\mathrm{tr},T}a_0^4\le1/1536\).
Next choose \(B_0\gg1\) so that
\(c_{\mathrm{tr},T}/B_0\le1/1536\), and set
\(\beta=a_0^2/B_0\).

Finally, decrease \(\nu_T\) so that
\begin{equation}\label{eq:linear-parameter-choice}
\nu_TT\le\frac{a_0^4}{16},
\qquad
c_{\mathrm{tr},T}a_0(\nu_TT)^{\frac14}\le\frac1{1536},
\qquad
\nu_T\le\nu_{T,\frac1{1024}},
\end{equation}
where the last constant is supplied by Lemma~\ref{lem:reaction}.

For these parameters,
\[
\mathcal D_{\mathsf{p}}(t)
\stackrel{\eqref{eq:weight-coercivity}}{\le}
-\frac1{128}\mathcal Q(t),
\qquad
\left\lvert \mathcal T_{\mathsf{p}}(t)\right\rvert
\stackrel{\substack{\eqref{eq:transport-total-bound}\\
\eqref{eq:linear-parameter-choice}}}{\le}
\frac1{512}\mathcal Q(t).
\]
Moreover, with \(\eta=1/1024\),
\[
\left\lvert \mathcal R_{\mathsf{p}}(t)\right\rvert-\frac1{1024}\mathcal K(t)
\stackrel{\eqref{eq:reaction-bound}}{\mathrel{\underset{T}{\lesssim}}}t\mathcal K(t).
\]
Since \(\mathcal K\stackrel{\eqref{eq:Q-def}}{\le}\mathcal Q\),
combining \eqref{eq:energy-parts}, \eqref{eq:weight-coercivity},
\eqref{eq:transport-total-bound}, \eqref{eq:reaction-bound}, and
\eqref{eq:linear-parameter-choice} yields
\[
\begin{aligned}
t\frac{d}{dt}E_{\mathsf{p}}(t)
+
\frac5{1024}\mathcal Q
-\left\lvert \left\langle f(t),w(t)\right\rangle_{\mathsf{p}(t)}\right\rvert
&\mathrel{\underset{T}{\lesssim}}
t\mathcal K
\\
&\stackrel{\eqref{eq:weighted-energy-comparable}}{\lesssim}
tE_{\mathsf{p}}(t).
\end{aligned}
\]
This proves
\eqref{eq:linear-weighted-estimate} with
\(\kappa_T=\frac5{1024}\).
<!-- end proof-36 -->
<!-- proposition-4: body_md -->
\label{prop:w2-apriori}
After decreasing \(\nu_T\), there is
\(\kappa_1>0\) such that, for almost every
\(t\in(0,T]\),
\begin{equation}\label{eq:w2-apriori}
\begin{aligned}
t\frac{d}{dt}E_{\mathsf{p}}(t)
+\kappa_1\mathcal Q(t)
\mathrel{\underset{T}{\lesssim}}{}&
t\bigl(E_{\mathsf{p}}(t)+E_{\mathsf{p}}(t)^2\bigr)
+t^{-1}\left\lVert \overline{w}_1(t)\right\rVert_{}^2
\\
&+
\nu t
+\sqrt\nu\,\left\lVert \overline{w}_1(t)\right\rVert_{}\mathcal K(t).
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
<!-- end proposition-4 -->
<!-- lemma-30: body_md -->
\label{lem:weight-pairing}
For the weight constructed above, one has, for every \(0<t\le T\) and
almost every \(\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\),
\begin{equation}\label{eq:grad-p-global}
\left\lvert \nabla\mathsf{p}(t,\xi)\right\rvert
\mathrel{\underset{T}{\lesssim}}
(1+\left\lvert \xi\right\rvert)\mathsf{p}(t,\xi),
\end{equation}
and, more sharply,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:nu-grad-p-small}
\nu\sqrt t\,\left\lvert \nabla\mathsf{p}(t,\xi)\right\rvert
\mathrel{\underset{T}{\lesssim}}
\sqrt\nu\,\mathsf{p}(t,\xi).
\end{equation}
Moreover, for each \(\gamma\in(\tfrac12,1)\),
\begin{equation}\label{eq:gaussian-pairing}
\left\lVert 
\mathsf{p}^{\frac{1}{2}}e^{-\frac{\gamma\left\lvert \xi\right\rvert^2}{4}}
\right\rVert_{L^2\cap L^4}
+
\left\lVert \mathsf{p}^{\frac{1}{2}}\nabla G\right\rVert_{L^2}
\mathrel{\underset{\gamma,T}{\lesssim}}
1.
\end{equation}
The composite corrector satisfies
\begin{equation}\label{eq:composite-profile-bounds}
\left\lvert w_{2,a}\right\rvert+\left\lvert \nabla w_{2,a}\right\rvert
\mathrel{\underset{\gamma,T}{\lesssim}}
e^{-\frac{\gamma\left\lvert \xi\right\rvert^2}{4}},
\qquad
\left\lvert v_{2,a}(\xi,t)\right\rvert
\mathrel{\underset{T}{\lesssim}}
\frac{1}{(1+\left\lvert \xi\right\rvert^2)^{\frac{3}{2}}}.
\end{equation}
<!-- end lemma-30 -->
<!-- proof-37: body_md -->
In the inner region \(\left\lvert \xi\right\rvert^2\le a_\nu(t)^2+1\),
\(\chi_\nu(t,\xi)\lesssim\left\lvert \xi\right\rvert(1+\left\lvert \xi\right\rvert)\); outside this region,
\(\nabla q=0\) almost everywhere.  Hence
\[
\begin{aligned}
\left\lvert \nabla\mathsf{p}\right\rvert
&\stackrel{\eqref{eq:p-def}}{\le}
\left\lvert \nabla\mathsf{p}_0\right\rvert+(\nu t)\left\lvert \nabla q\right\rvert
\stackrel{\substack{\eqref{eq:p0-gradient}\\
\eqref{eq:normalized-q-bounds}}}{\mathrel{\underset{T}{\lesssim}}}
(1+\left\lvert \xi\right\rvert)\mathsf{p}_0
\stackrel{\eqref{eq:p-comparable}}{\lesssim}
(1+\left\lvert \xi\right\rvert)\mathsf{p},
\end{aligned}
\]
which proves \eqref{eq:grad-p-global}.  Also,
\[
\mathsf{p}^{\frac{1}{2}}e^{-\frac{\gamma\left\lvert \xi\right\rvert^2}{4}}
\stackrel{\eqref{eq:p-global-bounds}}{\le}
\sqrt2e^{-\frac{(2\gamma-1)\left\lvert \xi\right\rvert^2}{8}},
\qquad
\mathsf{p}^{\frac{1}{2}}\left\lvert \nabla G\right\rvert
\stackrel{\eqref{eq:p-global-bounds}}{\lesssim}
